## 2026-08-22 — QA cleanup pass

Closes the three findings of the 2026-08-21 temporal QA pass (`docs/qa_report_21_aug_2026/`), which returned PASS WITH ISSUES with no correctness blocker. Deliberately bounded: no product feature, no Progress redesign, no dependency, no data migration, and no change to FSRS, the mature-only retention definition, the `—` empty state, Review history or the review queue snapshot. The QA report is kept as historical evidence and was not rewritten.

**D230. An observed retention bucket with no observed neighbour is a real measurement and is drawn as a point.** D227 established that nulls break the line; the implementation of that rule discarded any run shorter than two points, and only the final known bucket carried a marker. In the QA dataset — 30-day range, three-day buckets, mature reviews on Aug 29–30 and again on Sep 11 — every observed run was a singleton, so the chart rendered zero `data-retention-segment` paths and one dot, hiding whole periods of real history. `buildRetentionGeometry` now makes one pass and returns both: runs of two or more become a path, runs of one become a `data-retention-point` marker. Nulls still break runs, so no line crosses a gap. The rejected alternative was special-casing markers inside the component, which would have let markers and lines drift apart.

**D231. The chart's projection lives in one module.** `retentionChartPath.ts` owns `CHART_WIDTH`/`CHART_HEIGHT`/`PAD_X`/`xFor`/`yFor` and `RetentionChart.tsx` imports them, replacing a verbatim second copy of all five. Isolated markers must land on exactly the coordinates the segments use; two copies of the projection is precisely how that stops being true.

**D232. One observation is still not a trend.** The `known.length < 2` empty state ("Not enough review history yet to chart a trend.") is unchanged, so a range containing a single observed bucket keeps the copy rather than drawing a lone dot on an otherwise empty grid. The singleton fix is about observations that appear *alongside* other data.

**D233. Streak duration copy has one formatter.** `formatDayCount` sits beside `computeStreak` in `src/domain/stats/streak.ts` — the module that already owns the one streak definition — and serves Progress's Current streak value, its Best footer (which read **"Best: 1 days"**) and Today's "N days in a row". A generic pluralization or localization layer was rejected as oversized for three call sites. `StreakBadge`'s "day streak" is a static label beneath a separate number, not a counted noun, and is unchanged.

**D234. `newId()` prefers `crypto.randomUUID()` and falls back to `crypto.getRandomValues()`, keeping one id format.** `randomUUID` is secure-context only, so a plain-HTTP LAN dev origin — the physical-phone testing path — has `crypto` but no `randomUUID`. Because the router eagerly imports the design-preview pages and their fixtures mint ids at module scope, that `TypeError` landed during router evaluation and blanked every ordinary route, not just `/design-preview`. `getRandomValues` carries no secure-context restriction, so the fallback assembles the same RFC 4122 v4 shape from the same CSPRNG: same format, same entropy, no dependency, no environment-specific id, and no change to persisted identity. `newId()` remains the only id convention; no caller learned about the branch.

**D235. `design-preview/fixtures.ts` keeps its runtime-generated ids.** Deterministic fixture ids would be marginally tidier, but the fixtures were never the defect — they only made a broken id seam visible early, and editing them would have concealed it. Option, item and step ids need only be unique within a card, and the production `ReviewSessionScreen` treats them as opaque. With D234 in place the fixtures evaluate on any origin.

**D236. The QA report's 390px Ordering observation is a transition artifact, not an overflow bug, and no CSS was changed.** Frame-by-frame sampling of `document.documentElement.scrollWidth` at 390×844 shows the review card's entrance animation decaying 400 → 390 over roughly 250ms and then holding 390 for the rest of a six-second window; the QA report's 391px reading is one sample from that curve. At rest no element extends past 390 on `/review`, `/progress` or Today, and keyboard drags, pointer drags and the graded state all measure exactly 390. The nav's right cluster sits outside 390 inside the header's own `overflow-x: auto` row, which is existing intentional behavior and does not scroll the document.

Verification: `npx vitest run` 588/588 across 75 files, `npx tsc -b --force` and `npm run lint` clean, `npm run build` successful with the unchanged chunk-size advisory. Chromium against the production preview build at 1440×900 imported a backup whose mature reviews produce one observed bucket, a five-bucket gap, then two adjacent observed buckets: the chart drew one point at x≈77, one segment from x≈493 to x≈563, and the emphasized final marker — a known point, a real gap and a later known run, with nothing bridging them. The same passes showed `Best: 1 day` and `Best: 2 days` on Progress and "2 days in a row" on Today. At 390×844 Progress fit the viewport and Ordering settled at 390 after keyboard drags, a pointer drag and submission. Id generation was exercised three ways — `localhost` with the native method, `localhost` with `randomUUID` deleted before any module evaluated, and a real insecure LAN origin (`http://192.168.0.30:4174`, `isSecureContext: false`) — each booting the app and persisting a valid v4 deck id. Zero console or page errors anywhere.

## 2026-08-22 — Replace-import data safety (audit P1-1)

Closes the one release blocker the 2026-08-22 release-readiness audit reproduced in Chromium: a backup file that passed preflight cleared all five stores and then failed mid-write, leaving an emptied library beside a raw IndexedDB `DataError`. The audit is kept as written and was **not** edited to hide the defect. Deliberately bounded to that finding: no DST work (P1-2), no auth change (P1-3), no Supabase pagination (P1-4), no review-persistence, PWA, Collection-migration or Roadmaps product change, no dependency, and no backup-format redesign.

**D237. The invariant is stated in terms of outcome, not of the bug that exposed it: if a Replace import does not complete successfully, the previously persisted workspace must remain intact.** The malformed roadmap was only the cheapest trigger. Quota exhaustion, an IndexedDB error, or a network/RLS failure produce the same loss through the same clear-then-write gap, so fixing validation alone would have left the hazard in place with a narrower entrance.

**D238. Draft and Roadmap entities are validated element-by-element, like cards, decks and ReviewLogs.** `parseBackup` gave those two arrays `Array.isArray` and nothing else — the comment in the file said so on purpose — and a roadmap with no `id` is an entity IndexedDB cannot key, so it reached `bulkPut` and threw after the clear. `assertValidDrafts`/`assertValidRoadmaps` check exactly what the production types require and no more: absent optional fields stay valid, and a present-but-non-array `roadmaps` is now rejected instead of being handed to `bulkPut`. Roadmap node/edge ids and `node.deckId` are checked for presence but deliberately **not** resolved, matching the reasoning that leaves `deck.parentId` unresolved (D-era rule in `architecture.md`): a roadmap whose deck was later deleted is a legitimate export. `fixtureDraft`/`fixtureRoadmap`/`fixtureReviewLog` joined `backupFixtures.ts` so those shapes stay compiler-checked, since `*.test.ts` is excluded from the typecheck.

**D239. `BACKUP_VERSION` stays 2.** Nothing about the persisted format changed. Stricter validation of an already-required entity shape is not a format change, `app: 'code-srs'` remains the legacy format marker, and `docs/prompts/ai-card-prompt.md` was not touched because the accepted JSON contract is unchanged.

**D240. The storage seam gained one operation and one capability, rather than the import layer orchestrating five stores.** `Repository` now exposes `replaceAll(snapshot)` / `mergeAll(snapshot)` plus `importGuarantee: 'transactional' | 'best-effort'`. Only a backend knows whether five stores can be written as one unit, so both the operation and the honesty about it belong there; `src/data/backup.ts` composes the snapshot and reports the guarantee, and no component or hook imports `db` or branches on which backend is live (the Settings section reads a single boolean, `canReplaceImport()`). The per-store `clear()`/`bulkPut()` members stay — they are still the right shape for everything that is not a whole-workspace write.

**D241. Dexie replace and merge are one real `db.transaction('rw', …)`, not a snapshot-and-restore.** The rejected alternative — export the old workspace, clear, write, and restore on failure — was rejected because the restore is itself a write that can fail, so it would have converted a guarantee into a hope. Inside the transaction the clears and writes run sequentially so the failing operation is unambiguous; any rejection aborts the scope and IndexedDB rolls the clears back itself. Merge was wrapped in the same transaction because it cost nothing once the boundary existed and a half-upserted merge is its own small mess. Proven by removing the transaction and confirming all four rollback tests fail, then restoring it.

**D242. Supabase Replace is refused, not emulated.** PostgREST has no transaction spanning requests: five deletes and five upserts are ten committed statements, and no client-side compensation makes them one. `SupabaseRepository.replaceAll` therefore throws before issuing a single request, `importGuarantee` is `'best-effort'`, and the Import / Export section renders Replace as a focusable `aria-disabled` option with the reason rather than hiding it or offering it with a warning. Merge stays available and additive. The alternative — a `replace_workspace` SQL function doing the delete + insert for all five tables in one transaction — is the correct production answer and is recorded in `TODO.md`; it was **not** written this pass because it cannot be executed or verified without a live project (§15 already records that the existing schema is unverified for the same reason), and shipping untested destructive SQL would trade a known limitation for an unknown one.

**D243. Import failure copy lives in one pure module and never claims more than the backend guaranteed.** `src/domain/io/importFailure.ts` carries `ImportFailure` (with `stage` and `workspaceUnchanged`) and `describeImportFailure`. A rejected file states that it was refused before anything was imported; a failed write on a transactional backend says the existing data is exactly as it was; a failed write on a best-effort backend says the data may be incomplete and never says it is safe. Raw IndexedDB text is never rendered. The UI decides nothing: it renders one string.

Verification: `npx vitest run` 637/637 across 78 files, `npx tsc -b --force` and `npm run lint` clean, `npm run build` successful with the unchanged chunk-size advisory. Chromium against the production preview build, disposable demo workspace: workspace A (`PRIOR USER DECK`) seeded, then the audit's own file — valid except `"roadmaps": [{ "title": "no id here" }]` — offered under Replace. It was refused before the confirm dialog was even raised, with `Roadmap 1 is missing a valid "id". This backup was rejected before anything was imported, so nothing was changed.`; `PRIOR USER DECK` was still present and `INCOMING DECK` absent. A valid Replace then succeeded and left only `INCOMING DECK`. Checked at 1440×900 and 390×844, zero console errors. The post-validation write failure is no longer reachable through the built UI, so its proof is `src/data/dexie/replaceAll.test.ts`, which forces a genuine IndexedDB key-path error at the first and the last store written and a non-IndexedDB rejection in the middle, and asserts the contents of all five stores afterwards.

## 2026-08-22 — Local calendar-day arithmetic (audit P1-2)

Closes the second reproduced finding of the 2026-08-22 release-readiness audit: `startOfDay()` produced a correct **local** midnight and every consumer then stepped or compared days by adding a fixed 86,400,000 ms, which is wrong on the two days a year a local civil day is 23 or 25 hours long. Ten reviews on ten genuinely consecutive local dates spanning 2026-10-25 in `Europe/Belgrade` reported `{ current: 4, best: 6 }`. The audit is kept as written and was **not** edited to hide the defect. Bounded to that finding: no auth change (P1-3), no Supabase pagination (P1-4), no review-persistence, PWA or scheduling change, no dependency.

**D244. Calendar days and elapsed time are two different concepts, and only the first one moved.** Nothing persisted changed: `ReviewLog.reviewedAt`, `Card.scheduling.due`, `durationMs` and every FSRS interval remain absolute epoch milliseconds, so the fix is retroactive for existing history and no migration exists or is needed. What changed is statistics/presentation bucketing — streak, pace, heat map, milestones, ranges, "Yesterday". Relative-time formatting that genuinely means elapsed duration was left alone on purpose: `cards/shared/format.ts` ("3h ago", "in 2 days"), `SuggestedSessionHero.formatNextDue` ("in 4 hours"), and `clusterSessions`' 30-minute gap.

**D245. `src/domain/stats/calendarDay.ts` is the one home for local calendar-day arithmetic, and `startOfDay` moved into it.** `dateRange.ts` re-exports `startOfDay` so existing imports keep resolving, and `DAY_MS` was **deleted** rather than left exported — every consumer of it was a calendar-day consumer, so leaving the constant in place would only have been an invitation to reintroduce the bug. The audit also found what it did not list: `reviewHistory.ts` carried a private duplicate `startOfDay` plus its own `DAY`, and `computeReviewSeries`/`computeRetentionSeries` stepped multi-day buckets by `bucketDays * DAY`, which slides a bucket edge (and the retention chart's date label) off local midnight across a transition. Both were converted.

**D246. The primitive is a calendar-day index, not a chain of `Date.setDate()` calls.** `localDayIndex(ms)` projects the *local* `getFullYear/getMonth/getDate` onto `Date.UTC(...)`, where a day is exactly 24 hours by definition and no offset rule applies. Adjacency then reads literally as `next === previous + 1`, which is what `computeStreak` and `deriveMilestones` need, and no comparison rounds or tolerates an hour of slack. `addCalendarDays` does use `setDate`, and re-normalizes with `setHours(0,0,0,0)` **after** the shift so a zone whose transition lands at midnight still yields a value `startOfDay` would produce. Bare date strings (`"2026-10-25"`) are never parsed — that syntax has UTC semantics in JS, which is the same class of bug in a different disguise. No date library was added; native `Date` is sufficient here.

**D247. Range semantics were preserved exactly, and only their arithmetic changed.** `from` inclusive, `to` exclusive at the start of the day after the last included day, `days` unchanged, and `previousPeriod` is still the immediately preceding equal-length window — now equal in *calendar days*, so the two windows abut with no phantom one-hour gap or overlap when a transition falls inside one of them. The date-range selector's meaning is untouched.

**D248. DST tests pin the timezone in-file and prove the pin took effect.** `src/test/timeZone.ts` sets `process.env.TZ` (Node re-resolves the zone on assignment) and returns a restore function each `*.dst.test.ts` registers with `afterAll`, so the change cannot leak into another file sharing the worker process. No timezone package was installed and `vitest.config.ts` was not changed. Each file first asserts the runtime really resolved CET/CEST, because a pin that silently failed would otherwise leave the suite testing the machine's own zone and passing for the wrong reason. Those files import the modules under test **dynamically**, after the pin: a static import is hoisted above it, and module-scope `Intl.DateTimeFormat` instances capture the zone at construction.

**D249. Fixtures that encoded the broken model were corrected rather than left as latent failures.** `StreakBadge.test.tsx`'s "fixture sanity" case asserted that consecutive fixtures are exactly `DAY` apart — itself false on both transition days — and now asserts calendar adjacency; `progressMetrics.test.ts` moved from `Date.now() - n * DAY` to a fixed local `NOW` with a `daysAgo(n)` calendar helper; `ProgressPage.test.tsx` places its review fixtures with `addCalendarDays` because the page reads the live clock and its streak assertions ("2 days", "Best: 2 days") would have failed on 2026-03-29 and 2026-10-25. This is **not** a claim to have identified the intermittent single-run flake recorded in `CURRENT_STATE.md` §16: those runs were on 2026-08-17 and 2026-08-22, neither a transition date, and no evidence ties the flake to these tests. Its status is unchanged.

Verification: `npx vitest run` 686/686 across 81 files, `npx tsc -b --force` and `npm run lint` clean, `npm run build` successful with the unchanged chunk-size advisory. The three new DST suites were run against the pre-fix modules first: 21 of their 30 cases failed, including the audit's own reproduction. Browser pass in Chromium against the dev server with a disposable demo workspace, `timezoneId: 'Europe/Belgrade'` and Playwright's `page.clock` pinning each virtual day (no production fake-clock code): 40 new cards imported through the real Import UI, then three real reviews graded through production `/review` on Oct 23, Oct 24 and Oct 25 2026. On Oct 25 — the 25-hour day — the nav badge, Today's Momentum ("3 days in a row") and Progress ("3 days", "Best: 3 days") all read 3, the pace chart showed `Friday 3, Saturday 3, Sunday 3` and the heat map's last three cells read `Fri Oct 23: 3 reviews | Sat Oct 24: 3 reviews | Sun Oct 25: 3 reviews`. Advancing to Oct 26 with no reviews kept all four surfaces at 3 (the grace day, across the transition that used to break it), added a zero Monday bucket, and dated the derived 3-day streak milestone **"Yesterday"**. Reviewing three cards on Oct 26 moved every surface to 4 together. Checked at 1440×900 and 390×844, zero console errors.

## 2026-08-22 — Authentication mode follows backend mode (audit P1-3)

Closes the third reproduced finding of the 2026-08-22 release-readiness audit. `isAuthenticated` was `session !== null || local !== null` and never consulted `isSupabaseConfigured`, while `getRepository()` selected its backend from that predicate alone. The two could therefore disagree about which mode Itera was in. Reproduced in Chromium against the dev server with dummy `VITE_SUPABASE_*` and a planted `itera.session`: `/progress` rendered the app shell against `SupabaseRepository` with no Supabase user, and `/login` redirected to `/`, so the only page that could have signed the visitor in was unreachable. The audit is kept as written and was **not** edited. Bounded to this finding: no change to P1-4 pagination, the Supabase Replace RPC, review persistence, PWA caching or dependencies.

**D250. There is one mode decision, and authentication is downstream of it.** `AuthProvider` reuses `isSupabaseConfigured` from `src/data/supabase/client.ts` — the same constant `getRepository()` reads — rather than testing the environment a second time. `isAuthenticated` is now `isSupabaseConfigured ? session !== null : local !== null`, and `identity` splits on the same predicate. No mode registry, config service or environment abstraction was introduced: the coupling that was missing was one predicate, and a framework would have been a second source of truth wearing a nicer name. Everything downstream already flowed from `isAuthenticated`/`identity`, so `RequireAuth`, `AuthGate`, `LoginPage`, `AccountMenu*` and `AccountSettingsPage` needed no edit at all. The account menu's "Stored in this browser" line became unreachable in Supabase mode by construction, which is why it was **not** refactored — the impossible state was removed at its source rather than papered over at the render site.

**D251. A stale local session is cleared, and the local workspace is not touched.** `AuthProvider` calls `clearLocalSession()` once during the Supabase bootstrap, through the existing seam — the `itera.session` key is still named in exactly one file. Only the auth record is removed. The learner's IndexedDB data (decks, cards, review logs, drafts, roadmaps) is deliberately left exactly as it is: it is not deleted, and it is not uploaded. Auto-migrating it would be a silent write of a whole workspace into a cloud account the user has not signed into yet, and deleting it would destroy data the app has no right to discard. "What should happen to browser-local learning data when an origin moves to the cloud" is a real product question and stays open in `TODO.md`; conflating it with an auth predicate would have answered it by accident. Verified in the browser: after the stale session is cleared, a planted IndexedDB database and its rows are still present and readable.

**D252. The local record is never adopted in Supabase mode, not merely discounted.** `useState(() => isSupabaseConfigured ? null : readLocalSession())` means `local` is `null` from the first render, so there is no frame in which a stale session renders the app authenticated before `getSession()` resolves and revokes it. Fixing only the predicate would have been enough for `RequireAuth`, but would have left that flash available to any future consumer reading `local` directly. `loading` still initializes to `isSupabaseConfigured` and `AuthGate` still blocks on it, so the loading contract is unchanged. `signInLocal`/`signInDemo` are no-ops in Supabase mode as well; `SignInPanel` already never calls them there, so this only stops a future caller minting a record that could never authenticate. `signOut` was left clearing **both** stores unconditionally — it is defensive, it costs nothing, and weakening it because only one mode is active would reintroduce exactly the class of leftover this entry is about.

**D253. Mocking `isSupabaseConfigured` as a getter is what made the Supabase branch testable at all.** `vitest.config.ts` blanks `VITE_SUPABASE_*` to keep the suite hermetic, and `isSupabaseConfigured` is a module-evaluation-time `const`, so before this pass **no test in the repo had ever executed the Supabase half of the auth path**. Both auth suites now `vi.mock('@/data/supabase/client')` with `isSupabaseConfigured` exposed as a getter over a `vi.hoisted` flag. Because `AuthProvider` reads the flag during render — state initializer, effect, callbacks, memo — never at module scope, one file can render both modes without `vi.resetModules()` or dynamic imports, and the mock defaults to local so all 16 pre-existing local-mode cases are untouched. The alternative (`vi.stubEnv` plus module resets) would have tested the real predicate but forced every case into a dynamic import for no behavioural gain.

**D254. Supersedes the sign-out clause of D134.** D134 recorded Sign out as activating only when `isSupabaseConfigured && session`; the code had since moved to `isAuthenticated`, and now means "the active mode has a session" — a local or demo one in local mode, a Supabase one in Supabase mode. D134 is otherwise unchanged and is not edited.

Verification: `npx vitest run` 693/693 across 81 files, `npx tsc -b --force` and `npm run lint` clean, `npm run build` successful with the unchanged chunk-size advisory. The 7 new cases were run against the pre-fix `AuthProvider` first: 3 failed, including the audit's own stale-session reproduction. Browser pass in Chromium at 1440×900 and 390×844. **Local mode** (no `VITE_SUPABASE_*`): `/progress` while signed out redirected to `/login` carrying the route, signing in with Remember me wrote `itera.session` to `localStorage` and returned to `/progress`, a reload preserved it, the account menu read `filip@itera.test / Stored in this browser`, and Sign out cleared both stores and returned to `/login`. **Supabase-configured simulation** (dummy `VITE_SUPABASE_URL`/`VITE_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY`, no project, planted stale `itera.session` plus a planted IndexedDB database): `/progress` redirected to `/login`, `/login` stayed on `/login` in magic-link form, no "Stored in this browser" text was rendered anywhere, `itera.session` was gone from both stores, and the planted database and its row were still readable. The valid-Supabase-session branch is covered by the deterministic mocked tests only — the project owner's Supabase project was deleted mid-development (`CURRENT_STATE.md` §15) and none was created for this pass.


## 2026-08-22 — Complete Supabase collection reads (audit P1-4)

Closes the fourth and last P1 of the 2026-08-22 release-readiness audit, and the only one that could not be reproduced: every read in `SupabaseRepository` issued a single unbounded `select` with no `.range()`, while a Supabase project caps each API response at its **Max rows** setting and reports the cap only in a `Content-Range` header. A cloud-mode workspace larger than that cap therefore got a short read presented as a successful one — worst of all in `exportBackup()`, which writes whatever the five reads returned into a file that looks entirely normal. The audit is kept as written and was **not** edited to hide the defect. Bounded to this finding: no Supabase Replace RPC (still refused, D242), no merge atomicity, no auth change, no PWA, review-persistence, RLS, schema, migration or dependency work, and no change to the `Repository` interface.

**D255. Pagination lives below the seam, and the seam never learns about it.** `Repository` gained no `page`, `offset`, `cursor` or "hasMore", and no hook, page or component changed. A module-private `selectAll` helper in `src/data/supabase/SupabaseRepository.ts` wraps the six collection reads (`crud.getAll`, `cards.getDue`, `cards.search`, `reviews.all`, `reviews.forCard`, `reviews.range`); `getById` stays a single `.maybeSingle()` row. The seam expresses product operations, not PostgREST mechanics, so "return all matching entities" is a promise the backend keeps rather than a caveat the caller has to remember. The helper takes a `(fromRow, toRow) => PromiseLike<Page<T>>` factory rather than a prepared builder, because a `PostgrestFilterBuilder` is a one-shot thenable and the filters belong to the call site.

**D256. Termination never infers "last page" from "page shorter than requested", because that is the same bug in a new place.** The obvious loop — request 1000, stop when fewer than 1000 come back — is correct only when the client's page size is at or below the server's cap. A project whose **Max rows** is 500 answers *every* request with 500 rows, so that loop stops on page one of every query and silently truncates exactly as before. `selectAll` instead advances the offset by however many rows actually arrived and stops on an **empty** page, which needs no server metadata and holds for any cap; additionally it stops early when `select('data', { count: 'exact' })` reports that the accumulated rows already cover the full matching set. The count is the whole matching total, not the page, so it is a real bound: it removes the trailing empty request in the common case and caps the loop, which is why no arbitrary "maximum N pages" guard was added. When no count comes back the loop degrades to empty-page termination and stays correct, at the cost of one extra request. Both paths are tested, including the exact-multiple case where there is no short page to stop on.

**D257. `PAGE_SIZE` is 500 and correctness does not depend on it.** It is a round-trip/payload tradeoff and nothing else — not the platform default 1000 copied because it is common, and not a product setting. It is module-private and untestable from outside on purpose; the tests drive multi-page behaviour by giving the fake server a cap of 3, which is the case that matters (`serverCap < PAGE_SIZE`) and which no value of `PAGE_SIZE` can rescue. Raising a project's Max rows may still be worth doing operationally, but it is explicitly **not** the fix: a documented server setting is not a code invariant, and the next project created would not inherit it.

**D258. Every paginated query carries a deterministic order with a unique tie-breaker.** Offset paging over an unordered or non-unique key may duplicate rows, skip them, or return inconsistent pages, so `getAll`/`search`/`reviews.all` order by `id`, `getDue` orders by `due` then `id`, and `forCard`/`range` order by `reviewed_at` then `id`. None of this changes what the user sees: the final in-memory sorts (`due` ascending, `updatedAt` descending) are untouched, and because JavaScript's sort is stable the added order actually *improves* Dexie parity — Dexie's `toArray()` already yields primary-key order and `between()` already yields index order, so ties now resolve identically on both backends rather than arbitrarily on one. Duplicate `reviewedAt` values straddling a page boundary are covered by their own test.

**D259. In-memory filtering was preserved rather than optimized away, and `getDue`'s `limit` stays in memory with it.** The temptation while touching these queries is to push text, tag, type or `limit` down into PostgREST. That would break the parity the seam promises, and for `limit` it would be a correctness bug: deck and tag filtering still runs client-side, so a server-side cut discards candidates the filters have never seen, and a search that stopped "once it had enough raw rows" would miss matches sitting on a later page. The sequence stays: read the complete server candidate set, map, apply the same Itera predicates and sort, then apply the Itera limit. Two tests exist purely to fail if someone optimizes this later.

**D260. A failed page fails the whole read.** `selectAll` throws on the first error rather than resolving with the pages it already has. An incomplete result that presents itself as complete is the entire defect being fixed here; returning a prefix on error would reintroduce it through a different door. Retry policy is deliberately absent — that is normal transport behaviour and belongs to the client, not to this loop.

**D261. The Supabase backend got a chainable fake client instead of a mocked pagination function.** `src/data/supabase/fakeSupabaseClient.ts` is a non-test module for the same reason `backupFixtures.ts` is one: `tsconfig.app.json` excludes `*.test.ts`, so a fake written inline in a test can drift from the real client and from `supabase/schema.sql` with nothing failing. It records each request's filters, order, range and count, so the tests assert the query the repository actually constructed — the recorded ranges `0-499`, `3-502`, `6-505` are the proof the loop advances by rows *received*, not by the size it asked for. It reproduces the two PostgREST behaviours that matter: a hard per-response cap applied after the requested range, and generated columns computed exactly as `schema.sql` declares them, including `suspended` being NULL when the key is absent. That last detail is the audit's separate P3 divergence, which this pass deliberately did **not** fix; the parity dataset sets `suspended` explicitly so no test quietly blesses either behaviour. Reverting the loop to short-page termination fails 24 of the 31 cases in the reads suite, which is how it was checked that the coverage is not vacuous.

Verification: `npx vitest run` 750/750 across 84 files, `npx tsc -b --force` and `npm run lint` clean, `npm run build` successful with the unchanged chunk-size advisory; the fake client is confirmed absent from every emitted bundle chunk. 57 new tests across three files: the pagination edge matrix (zero rows, under a page, exactly one page, full + partial, several full + partial, exact multiple with and without a count, duplicate sort keys, later-page failure, and the single-row read staying unpaginated), the review-log and card read semantics at more than one page, the real `exportBackup()` against a row-capped cloud repository, and a parity suite reading one dataset through a real `DexieRepository` and a capped Supabase one and comparing the results outright across 8 search queries, 6 due queries and 7 review-range queries. Browser pass in Chromium at 1440×900 and 390×844 against the **local Dexie backend**, which is what the repository change had to leave alone: a 46-card / 2-deck / 60-log workspace imported through the real Settings UI, then Library and in-deck search, Today (30 due, 12-day streak, 75% retention), Progress (KPIs, heat map, retention chart, deck performance, milestones) and Review history all rendered correctly with zero console errors, and Export JSON round-tripped all 46 cards, 2 decks and 60 review logs with every log id present. **The pagination loop is code- and test-verified but has never run against a live PostgREST service** — the project owner's Supabase project was deleted mid-development (`CURRENT_STATE.md` §15) and none was created for this pass, so a real Max rows cap has never truncated a real response here. That remaining verification is recorded in `TODO.md`.

## 2026-08-22 — P2 release hardening (audit §10 items 3, 4, 7, 8, 9, 11)

Closes six of the twelve P2 items ranked in the 2026-08-22 release-readiness audit, after all four P1s were closed in their own passes. Each is a credible robustness, accessibility or deployment defect and none carries a product decision. The audit is kept as written and was **not** edited. Bounded to these six: no review-persistence error UI, no scheduling/ReviewLog atomicity, no Supabase Replace RPC, no merge atomicity, no live Supabase verification, no `suspended=NULL` work, no dependency bumps, no bundle splitting, no dark mode, no Settings sections, no Collection/Deck migration. **Review persistence (audit §10 items 5 and 6) is explicitly still open and is a separate pass.**

**D262. The Supabase session bootstrap always ends in a decided state, and a failure is signed-out *with a reason*.** `AuthProvider`'s `getSession()` was a bare `.then`, so a rejection left `loading` true and `AuthGate` on "Loading…" forever - the one path in the app to a permanently blank screen. It is now `.then`/`.catch`/`.finally` under a `cancelled` flag the effect cleanup sets, and it treats all four outcomes: a session, no session, a resolved `error`, and a rejection. The last two are handled identically because they are the same event to the user - GoTrue returns some failures and throws others, and a fix that only caught one of them would be half a fix. No session is ever invented on failure and `clearLocalSession()` still runs first, so a broken bootstrap cannot admit anyone; the P1-3 mode predicate, the auth-state subscription, `signOut` and local mode are untouched.

**D263. The failure message is one sentence on the existing Login alert, not a global error surface.** `AuthValue` gained exactly one field, `sessionError: string | null`, and `SignInPanel` renders `errors.form ?? sessionError` in the `role="alert"` paragraph it already had for magic-link failures. No error boundary, no error store, no auth-error route: the visitor is already being sent to `/login` by `RequireAuth`, and the only thing missing was a sentence explaining why. A live form error from the current session takes precedence over the bootstrap message, because it is the more recent thing the user did. The text is deliberately **not** the raw Supabase/transport message - "TypeError: Failed to fetch" on a first paint tells a learner nothing actionable. (The raw `signInWithOtp` message is still shown verbatim; that is pre-existing and out of this pass.) A session arriving later on the auth-state channel clears the error, so a magic-link return after a flaky start is not left displaying a stale complaint.

**D264. `workbox.globPatterns` is set explicitly, and `includeManifestIcons` is turned off to pay for it.** `vite-plugin-pwa` set no `workbox` key, so workbox-build's default `js`/`css`/`html` glob applied and the generated precache manifest was 9 entries: none of the twelve bundled Inter/JetBrains Mono `.woff2` files and not the Today hero's 230 KB PNG mask. The installed PWA therefore came back online rendering in `system-ui` without its imagery, contradicting `CURRENT_STATE.md` §19 in writing. The glob is now `**/*.{js,css,html,woff2,png,svg}` - the asset types this build actually emits and the app actually uses, not a catch-all, and `wasm` was left out because nothing emits any. Adding `png` made `itera-logo.png` match the glob *and* still be injected by `includeManifestIcons`, producing two byte-identical precache entries for it, so that option is now `false`; the glob covers every manifest icon in `dist/`. Result: 24 unique entries, 2404 KiB. **No `runtimeCaching` was added** - Supabase and auth requests must keep hitting the network - and `registerType: 'autoUpdate'`, `skipWaiting`, `clientsClaim`, `cleanupOutdatedCaches` and the single `NavigationRoute` are byte-for-byte what they were, because the audit found the update strategy sound.

**D265. `collectionPathFor` is cycle-safe by the same `seen`-set idiom the deck tree already uses, and truncates rather than throws.** `buildDeckTree`, `subtreeIds` and `buildCollectionTree` were all already cycle-safe; this one breadcrumb helper walked `parentId` with an unguarded `while (current)` and `path.unshift`. Deck parents are deliberately not validated referentially on import, so a hand-edited backup could hang the tab - not reachable through the UI, which excludes a deck's own subtree from its parent list. The guard stops at the first repeated collection, so a broken graph yields a short breadcrumb instead of a frozen page; throwing would have taken down a page over a display detail. Acyclic and missing-parent behaviour is unchanged, nothing is mutated, and **no deck-cycle rejection was added to backup import** - that is a data-contract change, not a rendering fix. Eight tests, four of which do not terminate at all against the previous implementation.

**D266. `DialogHost` contains and returns focus; a destructive confirm opens on Cancel.** Every confirm/prompt/alert in the app (17 invocations across 7 files) goes through this one component, which took focus on open but never contained Tab and never gave focus back. Three changes, all inside `DialogHost`: Tab/Shift+Tab wrap within the dialog's own controls and pull focus back in when it has escaped; the opener is captured before focus moves and restored on unmount under the same two-part guard `FloatingPanel` uses (still connected, and the user has not moved focus themselves); and a `danger` confirm now opens on **Cancel** rather than on the red action, so Enter on a dialog the user did not expect dismisses instead of deleting. Prompt, alert and non-destructive confirm keep the focus target they have always had. `DialogProvider`, the `Request` union, `settle`, the promise contract, the portal, the scrim, click-outside, Escape and every class name are untouched - `dialogs` was not replaced with a dependency and was not redesigned.

**D267. Containment is a `document` listener and an index lookup, not a React handler and `Node.contains`.** Tab is handled on the same `document` keydown that already owns Escape, because a handler on the portal root only sees events that originate inside it - and the case worth defending against is focus already sitting *behind* the modal. Position is then resolved with `items.indexOf(document.activeElement)` over the dialog's own focusable list, which answers "is focus inside" and "which end is it at" in one lookup and needs no containment test. That second detail is not only taste: `Node.contains` returns false for a genuine descendant under `happy-dom` inside the portal, and `active instanceof HTMLElement` is false there too because the test DOM's element classes are not the module's globals. Both were silently swallowing every Tab in a first implementation that passed the forward-wrap test by accident. The code that ships avoids both, so the tests measure the behaviour rather than the environment.

**D268. `downloadText` follows the conventional anchor lifecycle.** It created an object URL, clicked an anchor that was never in the document, and revoked the URL in the same task. That works in Chromium and is the pattern that has historically no-opped in Firefox and Safari, and Export JSON is the app's **only** backup path, so a silent failure there is the most expensive silent failure available. It now appends the anchor, clicks, removes it, and revokes on the next task via `setTimeout(…, 0)` - deferred so the browser can consume it, not left alive. No dependency, no change to the file contents, filename or backup format. Tested on fake timers so the schedule is asserted rather than waited on.

**D269. `schema.sql` becomes re-runnable without any semantic change.** The four `create policy "own rows"` statements for `cards`/`decks`/`drafts`/`review_logs` had no `drop policy if exists` guard, so re-running the consolidated script against an existing database aborted at the first with `42710` - while every `create table`/`create index` above them was already `if not exists`. Each now carries the guard, exactly as the later roadmaps block and `migrations/0002_single_card_model.sql` already did; those were the convention and these four were the outliers. The diff is purely additive: not one character of a policy predicate, role, `for all`, `using`, `with check`, `enable row level security` or `grant` changed. No historical migration file was touched and nothing was run against a database - the project's Supabase instance no longer exists (`CURRENT_STATE.md` §15).

Verification: `npx vitest run` 790/790 across 86 files, `npx tsc -b --force` and `npm run lint` clean, `npm run build` successful with the unchanged chunk-size advisory. 40 new tests, each set run against the unfixed module first: 9 of 14 dialog cases fail, 6 of 9 auth cases, 2 of 8 download cases, and 4 of 8 cycle cases do not terminate at all. The generated `dist/sw.js` was read rather than inferred: 24 precache entries including all 12 `.woff2`, `assets/itera-symbol-mask-*.png`, `itera-logo.png`, `favicon.svg`, `icon.svg`, the CSS, the four JS chunks, `index.html`, `registerSW.js` and `manifest.webmanifest`, with no duplicates and one `registerRoute` (the navigation route). Browser pass in Chromium at 1440x900 and 390x844: a destructive delete-deck confirm opened on Cancel, six Tabs and six Shift+Tabs stayed inside the dialog at both viewports, Escape closed it, the deck survived, and the console was clean; the New Deck prompt returned focus to its button; a real Export JSON produced `itera-backup-2026-08-22.json` containing a valid `app: 'code-srs'` envelope with the deck, left no anchor in the DOM and raised no error. **The auth fix was verified against a real supabase-js rejection, not only mocks:** with dummy `VITE_SUPABASE_*` pointing at an unreachable host and an expired stored token planted, `/progress` sat on "Loading…" for ~27 s while GoTrue exhausted its own refresh retries and then landed on `/login` in magic-link form with the alert rendered and `itera.session` cleared - where the pre-fix build stays on "Loading…" indefinitely. That ~27 s is supabase-js's internal backoff, not Itera's, and was left alone. One gap the pass does not close is recorded in `TODO.md`: a dialog opened from a row kebab menu has no opener to return focus to, because those menus deliberately run without `FloatingPanel`'s `manageFocus`.

## 2026-08-22 — Atomic review persistence and a recoverable Review failure state (audit §10 items 5 and 6)

Closes the last two ranked P2 items and the last open finding of the 2026-08-22 release-readiness audit. They are one pass because they are one defect seen from two sides: grading wrote two stores as two operations, and the UI had no way to represent the write failing. The audit is kept as written and was **not** edited. Bounded to these two: no Review redesign, no change to the grading model or to FSRS, no mid-session undo, no multi-level undo, no Supabase Replace RPC (still refused, D242), no merge atomicity, no `suspended=NULL` work, no PWA, no dependency, no bundle splitting, no Collection/Deck migration.

**D270. The invariant is stated as an outcome, not as the bug: a submitted review's Card scheduling and its ReviewLog are committed together, or neither is committed.** `usePersistReviewResult` did `await repo.cards.put(graded)` then `await repo.reviews.append(log)`. The audit's framing - "if the log write fails the card has advanced with no history row" - understates it, because every stat in the app derives from the logs: Progress, mature retention, the streak, the activity heat map and Today's pace all go blind to a review whose card behaves as though it happened, and nothing can reconstruct it afterwards. Reversing the order was rejected outright: it produces the opposite inconsistency (a log for a review the card never took), not a fix. Compensating rollback was rejected for the reason D241 already recorded for import - the compensating write can fail too, which converts a guarantee into a hope.

**D271. The operation went onto the storage seam, next to `replaceAll`/`mergeAll`, rather than into the hook.** `Repository` gained `commitReview({card, log})`, `revertReview({card, logId})` and `reviewGuarantee: WriteGuarantee`. The argument is D240's, unchanged: only a backend knows whether two stores can be written as one unit, so both the operation and the honesty about it belong there. `ImportGuarantee` is now an alias of the renamed `WriteGuarantee` - the capability was never specific to import, and `importGuarantee` keeps its name because it says which operation it is about. The per-store `cards.put` / `reviews.append` / `reviews.delete` members stay; they are still the right shape for card editing, import and export. No hook or component sequences the two stores any more, and paging, transactions and RPC names remain entirely below the seam.

**D272. Dexie is one real `rw` transaction over `cards` + `reviewLogs`, scoped narrower than `workspaceWrite`.** A review must not block, or be blocked by, writes to decks, drafts or roadmaps, so it gets its own two-store scope rather than reusing the import transaction. The log write stays `add`, not `put`: a log id that already exists means the caller is re-recording something already committed, and aborting is the honest answer rather than silently overwriting history. Proven non-vacuous by replacing the transaction with two bare awaits and confirming three rollback cases fail - the log-write-fails-after-card case, the duplicate-id case, and the failed-undo case - then restoring it.

**D273. Supabase got a database function, where the Replace RPC of D242 deliberately did not.** Hiding two PostgREST requests behind a method named `commitReview` and calling the finding closed was the outcome to avoid; two requests are two commits no matter what the client calls them. `supabase/migrations/0004_review_commit_rpc.sql` adds `commit_review(text, jsonb, text, jsonb)` and `revert_review(text, jsonb, text)`, each doing its update and its insert/delete inside PostgREST's per-request transaction, so a raise or a constraint violation anywhere rolls both back. This one was written blind while `replace_workspace` still is not, and the difference is not confidence but blast radius: this function creates nothing, drops nothing and deletes no row a caller did not name, its rollback is two `drop function` statements, and a bug in it fails a single grade loudly. `replace_workspace` clears five tables. Untested destructive SQL remains the trade D242 refused.

**D274. `SECURITY INVOKER`, so RLS stays the ownership check.** The body runs as the caller, so the existing `own rows` policies apply unchanged: the card update matches zero rows for a card the caller does not own, and `if not found then raise` turns that into a rollback. `SECURITY DEFINER` would have meant re-implementing the same check by hand against `auth.uid()` for no gain and with a real chance of getting it wrong, so it was not used. `search_path` is pinned to `public, pg_temp` regardless; `user_id` is still filled by the column default and is never sent by the client; `execute` is revoked from `PUBLIC` (Postgres grants it there by default on a new function) and granted only to `authenticated`, matching every table grant, with `anon` left out. No table, policy, index or existing grant was touched, and the service role is not involved anywhere.

**D275. The commit is idempotent on the network, and the client is idempotent by object identity.** The function's insert is `on conflict (id) do nothing`, because a commit that reached Postgres but whose response was lost leaves the client believing it failed - and a retry must not become a second review. Since a primary-key conflict is resolved by the index and not filtered by RLS, an RLS-scoped `if not exists` after it proves the row that is now there is the caller's. Dexie deliberately does **not** mirror this: there, a resolved promise means committed, so a retry cannot race a hidden success, and `add`'s duplicate rejection is a useful caller-bug alarm rather than a hazard. On the client side, retry safety comes from re-sending the same object, not from a flag: `reviewService.submit` mints the log id at grade time and the Review shell keeps that one `SubmitReviewResult` for the card's lifetime. `usePersistReviewResult` also now derives the graded card's `updatedAt` from `log.reviewedAt` rather than `Date.now()`, so every attempt writes a byte-identical card. **One grade produces one immutable result; a retry persists that result rather than grading again.**

**D276. The phase machine gained one state, because a failed write must not land where a successful one does.** `rate()` used to dispatch `GRADED` *before* persistence and then call `onGraded?.(result)` without awaiting it, so a rejection was an unhandled promise rejection while the session sat in `transitioning` with the ratings disabled and the index never advanced - frozen, silent, and indistinguishable from success. `onGraded` is now awaited, `GRADED` is dispatched only once it resolves, and a rejection dispatches the new `PERSIST_FAILED` (`rating -> persistFailed`), with `RETRY_PERSIST` going back. Every transition keeps the source-state guard the existing four already had, which is what makes a second retry click a no-op without a single new boolean. A full state-machine rewrite was considered and rejected: one member and two actions expressed everything needed.

**D277. The failure surface is an inline alert with one action, not a dialog and not a redesign.** `ReviewPersistError` replaces the rating controls' note slot: `role="alert"`, the message, and a **Try again** that takes focus on entry because Review is keyboard-first and the rating buttons have just gone dead. The ratings stay disabled - the learner has already answered and already chosen a grade, and the retry re-sends that same result, so offering a fresh grade would be offering a different review. Exiting stays on Escape and the top bar's X; a second "Leave session" button was considered and left out as a duplicate affordance. A `useDialogs()` modal was rejected because `/review` is deliberately chrome-free and a modal would pull focus out of the card. The same pass fixed the note that slot used to carry: "Preview only - nothing recorded." was displayed during `transitioning`, which in a real session is exactly the window in which the grade *is* being recorded. It is now gated on the caller having no `onGraded` at all, so only the preview routes it was written for ever see it.

**D278. Failure copy lives in one pure module and never promises more than the backend guaranteed.** `src/domain/review/reviewPersistFailure.ts` is D243's rule applied to the second atomic operation: it takes a `WriteGuarantee` and returns a sentence. Transactional says nothing was changed, which is true here and is the reassurance worth giving; best-effort says part of it may have been recorded and never says the progress is safe. That branch is unreachable today - both backends are transactional - and exists so the copy cannot silently become a lie if a backend regresses. Raw IndexedDB, PostgREST, SQL and network text never reach the DOM, and the message carries no "try again" sentence because the button is beside it (the undo message, which has no button, does name its next step).

**D279. Undo was pulled in because it is the exact inverse of the operation being fixed.** `useUndoGrade` had the identical two-write shape and could leave a card restored with its log still present, or the reverse. It now goes through `revertReview`, transactional on both backends. `undoLast` also gained the `try`/`catch` it never had, so a failed undo leaves the stack and the index alone rather than desynchronising the UI from storage, and the completion screen says the review is still recorded. The delete inside `revert_review` is deliberately *not* checked for a match, so an undo retried after its delete committed succeeds instead of failing on a row that is already gone. Nothing else about undo changed: still one level, still only on the "All done" screen, still no history redesign.

**D280. `useGradeCard` was deleted.** Nothing in `src` imported it; it computed FSRS a second time and carried the exact two-write sequence this pass removed, so leaving it would have left a working path back to the bug for whoever wired it up next. `docs/itera-migration-plan.md` Phase 3 named it as a dual-write site for the CardState extraction that migration `0002` abandoned, and that line is corrected rather than left pointing at a deleted symbol.

Verification: `npx vitest run` 825/825 across 90 files, `npx tsc -b --force` and `npm run lint` clean, `npm run build` successful with the unchanged chunk-size advisory. 35 new tests across four files - Dexie transaction rollback at either store plus a duplicate log id and unrelated data left untouched (9), the Supabase RPC call contract including that a commit issues **no** table writes at all and that a repeated commit stays one log (9), the whole failure UX through the real session against fake-indexeddb (9), and copy that matches the guarantee (5) - plus 3 phase-machine cases. Both sabotage checks were run: removing the Dexie transaction fails 3 of the 9 transaction cases, and restoring the pre-fix "dispatch GRADED then fire-and-forget" ordering fails 5 of the 9 UI cases. Browser pass in Chromium at 1440x900 and 390x844 against the local Dexie backend, on a disposable three-card workspace: a mouse grade and two keyboard grades each advanced with exactly one log written and the card's `reps` and `due` moved, "All done" rendered, `/progress/history` showed exactly the three reviews, completion Undo restored the third card to `reps: 0` and its original `due` while removing exactly one log, and re-grading it produced one log again - zero console errors throughout. The failure state was driven in the same browser by an init script that makes the `reviewLogs` store's `add` throw (harness-only; **no production failure switch was added**): the session stayed on card 1 with the answer still revealed, the alert rendered at both viewports with focus on **Try again**, IndexedDB held zero logs and `reps: 0`, and pressing Enter on the focused button committed the identical result - one log, rating 3, `reps: 1`. **`0004_review_commit_rpc.sql` has never run against a live Postgres**; the fake client models the function's semantics but is not the function, and the specific checks a real project must settle are recorded in `TODO.md`.

## 2026-08-22 - The repository becomes an npm workspace, and `@itera/core` takes ownership of the entity contracts

First implementation step of the web + native convergence work (Phase 1, steps 1.0 and 1.1 of the Phase 0 architecture report). Deliberately the smallest possible structural change: a workspace, one shared package, and the type layer moved into it. **No domain logic, no repository code, no hooks, no auth, no UI and no persisted contract moved or changed**, and no Expo/React Native code exists. The point of the step is to prove that one shared TypeScript package resolves identically in TypeScript, Vite and Vitest before anything valuable is moved into it.

**D281. The web app stays at the repository root for now; only `packages/*` became a workspace.** The alternative - move the web app into `apps/web` in the same pass - front-loads the largest diff into the step with the least information, and if resolution breaks there is no way to tell whether the move or the extraction caused it. Splitting the root `package.json` into a workspace root plus a web package is a separate, later, separately-verifiable step. Until then the root package is still the web application and still owns every existing dependency; none were moved, added, removed or upgraded.

**D282. `@itera/core` is consumed as TypeScript source, with no build step.** `main` and `types` both point at `src/index.ts`. Vite compiles it through its normal source pipeline, Vitest transforms it natively, and Metro will do the same through `babel-preset-expo`. A `tsc -b` emit step would add a permanent watch process to every developer's loop and a class of stale-output failures that are hard to read, in exchange for nothing this repository needs. The cost is that consumers typecheck core's source rather than a `.d.ts`, which is a benefit rather than a cost: an error in core fails the consumer's build too.

**D283. Runtime resolution is the npm workspace symlink, not a bundler alias, and TypeScript needs no `paths` entry for it either.** `node_modules/@itera/core` links to `packages/core`, so ordinary package resolution answers `import ... from '@itera/core'` the same way in all three tools. Adding `resolve.alias` to `vite.config.ts` and a matching entry to `vitest.config.ts` was considered and rejected: two aliases that must agree with each other, with `tsconfig` `paths`, and later with Metro's resolver, is four places to drift. **Neither `vite.config.ts` nor `vitest.config.ts` was touched in this pass**, which is the evidence that one mechanism was enough. The existing `@` alias is unrelated and unchanged.

**D284. The package manifest has no `exports` map and exactly one entry point.** A single barrel means no consumer can reach a path inside the package, so the internal file layout stays free to change, and it keeps resolution boring for Metro, whose `exports` handling has varied across Expo SDK versions. `packages/core/src/index.ts` is literally the former `src/types/index.ts`, moved - which is why it is `export *` per module rather than a maintained name list. Unlike that barrel it re-exports values as well as types: `richText` and `CARD_SCHEMA_VERSION` are part of the card contract, and a shared package that omitted them would force every consumer to reach past it.

**D285. `src/types/` survives as a re-export shim, so relocating the contracts and rewriting ~145 files' imports are not the same commit.** Four files (`index.ts`, `card.ts`, `review.ts`, `common.ts` - the only specifiers anything imports) now re-export from `@itera/core` and **define nothing**. `@/types` deliberately keeps `export type *`, exactly what it did before, so it still exposes types only and the two runtime values still come from `@/types/card`; preserving the surface byte-for-byte is what makes this a relocation rather than a behaviour change. `deck.ts`, `draft.ts` and `roadmap.ts` got no shim because nothing ever imported them directly. The shim layer is transitional: new code imports `@itera/core`, and the layer is removed once the web app's imports have been migrated.

**D286. The "exactly one implementation" rule is enforced at runtime, not by review.** `src/types/coreSurface.test.ts` asserts that `richText` imported from `@itera/core` is the *same function reference* as the one re-exported through `@/types/card`. A shim that redefined a contract, or a resolver that loaded the package twice, would still typecheck and still behave correctly in isolation, then hand the two platforms two implementations of one contract - the precise failure the shared package exists to prevent. Reference equality is the only check that catches it, and it can only be made at runtime. Confirmed non-vacuous: rewriting the shim to redefine `richText` fails the identity case with `expected [Function richText] to be [Function richText]`.

**D287. `tsconfig.core.json` compiles core with no DOM and no ambient `types`, which makes platform neutrality a build failure rather than a convention.** `lib` is `["ES2023"]` and `types` is `[]`, so a `window.`, `document.` or `process.` reference in core fails `npx tsc -b --force` here rather than surfacing later as a Metro/Hermes runtime error in a React Native app. Verified by adding `window.location.href` to a core module and watching the gate fail with `TS2304: Cannot find name 'window'`. The new `tsconfig.base.json` holds only the ten options `tsconfig.app.json` and `tsconfig.node.json` already shared, copied unchanged - `tsc -p tsconfig.app.json --showConfig` resolves to the same options as before, so this is de-duplication and not a strictness change. Core is a third project reference, so it is checked whether or not a consumer happens to import a given file; confirmed non-vacuous by planting a type error in core and watching the gate fail.

Verification: `npx vitest run` 828/828 across 91 files, `npx tsc -b --force`, `npm run lint` and `npm run build` all clean. The count rose from 825/90 by exactly the three cases of the new resolution guard; **no existing test was moved, renamed or edited**, because only type modules relocated and the shim keeps every `@/types*` specifier valid. `git diff --summary` records all six moved type files as **100% renames** - byte-identical content - and no file outside `src/types/`, the tsconfig set and the two package manifests changed at all. The lockfile diff is three hunks and no dependency churn: the `workspaces` declaration, the `node_modules/@itera/core` link entry and the `packages/core` entry; `npm audit fix` and `npm update` were not run and no version moved. `npm run build` emitted **byte-identical bundle hashes** to the pre-change baseline (`index-CSFGrn07.js`, `index-f7Qmf3Gv.css`, 24 precache entries), which is the strongest available evidence that the restructure changed no behaviour.

Browser pass in Chromium against the local Dexie backend on a 6-deck / 40-card workspace imported through the app's own Merge importer: Today, Library, a Collection, a leaf Deck, card study, card edit, card create, deck flip-through preview, a deck-scoped Review, Progress, Review history and Settings all rendered at 1440x900 and 390x844 with **zero console errors, zero page errors and zero failed requests**, and a real Space reveal in `/review` produced the rating controls with live FSRS intervals (1m / 6m / 10m / 8d), exercising the relocated `SchedulingState` through `previewNextStates` at runtime. A grep of `packages/core/src` for `window`, `document`, `localStorage`, `sessionStorage`, `indexedDB`, `import.meta`, `react-router`, `dexie`, `dnd-kit`, `lucide`, `react-dom`, `tailwind`, `process.` and `navigator` returns nothing but one pre-existing comment naming CodeMirror as an example source of language ids; core declares no dependencies and imports no module outside itself. **React was deliberately not added to core's manifest**: this step contains types only, and the eventual peer-dependency decision belongs with the step that moves the hooks, when Expo's pinned React version is actually known.

## 2026-08-22 - `@itera/core` takes ownership of the platform-neutral domain engine

Phase 1 Step 1.2 of the web + native convergence work, on top of 1.0 (the workspace) and 1.1 (the entity contracts). All 40 `src/domain/` modules moved into `packages/core/src/domain/`, along with the `Repository` interface, `src/lib/{id,shuffle}.ts`, the DST timezone test helper, and seven pure helpers that happened to live under `features/`. **No algorithm changed and no persisted shape changed.** The scheduler, `buildReviewLog`'s required `stateBefore`, `reviewService.submit`'s compute-once semantics, mature-retention eligibility, the DST-safe calendar day, all five graders, the backup envelope and `CARD_SCHEMA_VERSION` are the same code in a different directory. What changed is ownership: a React Native client will consume these rather than re-derive them, which is the entire mitigation for the ten silent-divergence risks the Phase 0 report ranks.

**D288. The web app keeps re-export shims at the old paths; ~130 files' imports were not rewritten.** Same trade as D285 for the types, one step larger: **47** files - 38 under `src/domain/*`, plus `src/lib/{id,shuffle}.ts`, `src/data/repository.ts` and six `features/` paths - now hold nothing but explicit `export { ... } from '@itera/core'` name lists, and `src/features/library/collectionTree.ts` is a 48th that is a shim apart from the two functions of D291. Explicit lists, not `export *`, so `@/domain/stats/streak` exposes exactly what it exposed before and a typo cannot silently resolve through a shim to an unrelated symbol. Rewriting every call site instead was rejected for this commit: it would bury a mechanical relocation inside a 130-file diff and make "did behaviour change?" unanswerable by inspection. The shims are transitional and each one is a one-line deletion later.

**D289. The barrel widened rather than gaining subpaths.** Every moved module is re-exported from `packages/core/src/index.ts`, so the package still has no `exports` map and exactly one entry point (D284) - the property that keeps Vite, Vitest and eventually Metro resolving it identically. Deep imports (`@itera/core/src/domain/...`) and a wildcard `exports` map were both considered and rejected as a second resolution mechanism to keep in sync. The cost is a large barrel, and it was checked rather than assumed: across the types, the repository contract, the domain, `lib/` and the seven helpers, exactly **one** exported name collided (see D290). A second barrel, `packages/core/src/types/index.ts`, exists only so core's own modules can write `from '../../types'` without importing the package barrel and creating a cycle.

**D290. `collectionTree`'s `leafDecks` was a delegation, and is now simply gone.** It was `export function leafDecks(decks) { return leafDecksOf(decks) }` - a rename of `domain/decks/tree.ts`'s function - which is the only name that appeared twice in the merged surface. Keeping both would have meant an ambiguous star export (silently dropped by ESM) or an alias in the barrel that no source file uses. The delegation is deleted, the barrel exports the deck-tree function, and the web shim re-exports it under the identical name, so every call site is unchanged and there is one implementation.

**D291. Collection *derivation* moved; the two `URLSearchParams` helpers did not.** `selectionToSearchParams`/`selectionFromSearchParams` encode a `LibrarySelection` into a browser query string. `URLSearchParams` is not typed under core's `lib: ["ES2023"], types: []`, and the fix would have been declaring a global for it - weakening the one mechanism that enforces platform neutrality (D287) to make an extraction easier, which is the wrong direction. Native does not reuse a query string either; Expo Router carries params. So the derivation functions and the `LibrarySelection` type are core's - there is still exactly one definition of what a selection is - and only its URL encoding stayed web-side, in a file that is otherwise a shim. Its two test cases moved to a small web test file beside it; the rest of `collectionTree.test.ts` moved to core with the code it covers.

**D292. `ObjectiveResult` lives with the graders, not with the phase machine.** `domain/grading/walkthrough.ts` imported it from `features/reviewV2/reviewPhase.ts` - a domain module reaching into the UI layer, and a `features/` path that could not follow the domain into a platform-neutral package. It is a grading contract, so it is now `packages/core/src/domain/grading/types.ts`; `reviewPhase.ts` re-exports it, which leaves its four importers and its own test untouched. This is the type-level half only. The `InteractionDefinition` split, which is the part that carries a `View: ComponentType`, is deliberately still Step 1.7.

**D293. The `Repository` interface moved early, and only the interface.** The six pure card-save modules import the `Repository` type and `reviewPersistFailure` imports `WriteGuarantee`, so leaving the contract in `src/data/` would have left core importing the web app - the one thing the package may never do. `src/data/repository.ts` is types only and has no runtime code at all, so moving it costs nothing and drags nothing. **Every implementation stayed**: `DexieRepository`, the Dexie schema, `SupabaseRepository`, the Supabase client, `getRepository()`, `src/data/backup.ts` and all seven hooks are untouched and remain Step 1.3/1.4. This is a narrowly scoped dependency move, not an early start on 1.3.

**D294. `newId()` describes the crypto it uses structurally, and reads it at call time.** Core has no DOM and no Node ambient types, so neither the `crypto` global nor the `Crypto` type exists there. Adding `"DOM"` to `tsconfig.core.json` for one type would have disabled the enforcement for the whole package, and importing a React Native polyfill into core would have made the shared package platform-specific. Instead `newId()` declares the two members it actually uses (`randomUUID?`, `getRandomValues`) as a local interface and reads them off `globalThis` **inside the function** - module scope would freeze whatever global existed at import, breaking both a late polyfill and the tests' `vi.stubGlobal`. Runtime behaviour is byte-identical: native `randomUUID()` when available, the secure-context-free `getRandomValues` v4 fallback otherwise, same format, same entropy. Native supplies `react-native-get-random-values` at its own entry point, with no change to this function.

**D295. Core's tests are typechecked, in a second project rather than by relaxing the first.** The web app has never typechecked its tests, and that choice was deliberately not copied: core is the code both platforms will run, so a core test drifting out of the build takes a shared contract's only regression net with it. `tsconfig.core.test.json` covers the same directory with `types: ["node"]` - the DST helper pins `process.env.TZ`, `id.test.ts` stubs `globalThis.crypto` - while `tsconfig.core.json` now excludes `*.test.ts` and `src/test/` and keeps `types: []`. `lib` omits DOM in both, so no core file, test or not, may touch a browser global. Core source is therefore checked twice, once with nothing ambient and once with Node, and `tsc -b` runs both. **It paid for itself immediately**: four tests in `domain/cards/*Form.test.ts` called `legacyMcqCardToForm`, `legacyOrderingCardToForm`, `legacyCardToForm` and `legacyWriteCodeCardToForm` - all deleted with the single-card-model convergence - and passed anyway, because `expect(() => undefinedFn()).toThrow()` is satisfied by the `ReferenceError`. They asserted nothing about the product and were deleted rather than ported. A fifth error was real narrowing (`record.interaction.comparison` on a `Card`-annotated literal) and was fixed with `satisfies`.

**D296. Platform neutrality gained an import check, because the compiler only catches globals.** `tsconfig.core.json` fails on `window`/`document`/`process` (D287) but `import Dexie from 'dexie'` typechecks there perfectly well and would only fail under Metro. `packages/core/src/platformNeutrality.test.ts` reads core's own sources and fails on a browser token, on any import from the web app's `@/` alias, on a self-reference to `@itera/core` (which would make the barrel circular), and on importing a package core's manifest does not declare. That last rule is why `ts-fsrs` was added to `packages/core/package.json` in this pass: core genuinely imports it now, and without the declaration it resolves off the workspace root's hoisted `node_modules` - working on web forever and failing on native later. It is the only dependency core declares, and no package was installed or upgraded to add it.

Verification: `npx vitest run` 831/831 across 93 files, `npx tsc -b --force` clean across four projects, `npm run lint` clean (0 warnings, 0 errors, 394 files, `packages/core` included), `npm run build` successful with the unchanged chunk-size advisory. The count arithmetic is in `CURRENT_STATE.md` §16: 34 of the 93 files now run from `packages/core`, +7 tests are the two new structural files plus two added identity cases, and -4 are the vacuous legacy tests of D295. Root `npx vitest run` discovers the core suites with **no config change** (`packages/core` is not under `node_modules/`), confirmed by counting 34 core + 59 web files rather than assuming. That the move was a move was measured, not asserted: of the 48 relocated **source** modules, 8 are byte-identical to their pre-move blobs and the other 40 differ **only** in import-specifier lines; of the 33 relocated test/support files, 8 are byte-identical, 21 differ only in imports, and the remaining 4 are exactly the `*Form.test.ts` files the vacuous-test deletion touched. `lib/id.ts` and `library/collectionTree.ts` are excluded from those counts because their content changes are the deliberate ones of D294 and D290/D291.

Five sabotage checks were run and reverted, each confirming a guard is non-vacuous: a `typeof window` in a core module fails both `platformNeutrality` and `tsc -b --force` (`TS2304: Cannot find name 'window'`); `import type { ID } from '@/types'` in core fails the web-alias case; `import Dexie from 'dexie'` fails both the token case and the undeclared-dependency case; `import { clsx } from 'clsx'` fails the undeclared-dependency case alone; and a shim that redefines `computeStreak` instead of re-exporting it fails `coreSurface.test.ts`'s reference-equality case. The forbidden-token sweep over all of `packages/core/src` returns only the guard test's own token list and the English word "window" in prose comments - zero runtime or import hits - and `grep` for imports from core back into `src/` returns nothing.

Browser pass in Chromium at 1440x900 and 390x844 against the local Dexie backend, on a disposable three-card / three-deck workspace: Today, Library, a Collection, a leaf Deck, card study, card create, card edit, deck flip-through preview, Progress, Review history and Settings Import/Export all rendered with **zero console errors, zero page errors and zero failed requests** at both viewports. The Review round trip is the evidence that matters, since this step moved the scheduler: a Space reveal produced the rating controls with FSRS intervals **1m / 6m / 10m / 8d**, identical to the pre-change baseline recorded in the previous entry; grading Good advanced the card from `reps: 0` to `reps: 1` with a moved `due`, wrote **exactly one** ReviewLog carrying `rating: 3` and `stateBefore: "new"`, and that review appeared in `/progress/history`. Undo reverted it to `reps: 0` with zero logs, so the transactional `revertReview` path is intact too.

`npm run build` emitted a main chunk of 1,072.05 kB (gzip 304.30 kB) against the pre-change 1,070.76 kB (gzip 304.77 kB): **+1.29 kB raw, -0.47 kB gzipped**, accounted for by the shim modules' wrappers. The CSS hash is unchanged (`index-f7Qmf3Gv.css`), the precache is still 24 entries, no fixture or test module appears in any chunk, and there is one `@itera/core` (a single workspace symlink; the reference-equality tests would fail on a second copy). The lockfile diff is one hunk declaring `ts-fsrs` under `packages/core`, with no version moved and nothing installed.

## 2026-08-23 - `@itera/core` takes ownership of the cloud backend and backup orchestration

Phase 1 Step 1.3 of the web + native convergence work. `SupabaseRepository`, the fake PostgREST server that tests it and the backend-neutral backup persistence orchestration moved into `packages/core`. **No algorithm changed and no persisted shape changed**: pagination, filtering, the due query, ReviewLog ranges, the two Review RPCs, the Replace refusal and every guard in front of an import are the same code in a different directory. What changed is where the environment is read. The `import.meta.env` boundary is now drawn once, at `src/data/index.ts`, and never crossed again - which is what makes the cloud backend reusable by an Expo client configured from `EXPO_PUBLIC_*`.

**D297. The Supabase client is a required constructor argument, and that single change is the whole portability fix.** `constructor(sb: SupabaseClient = getSupabase())` was the only thing binding the class to the web app. A default parameter is evaluated lazily but the *import* is not: `import { getSupabase } from './client'` was unconditional, so every consumer of the repository pulled `import.meta.env` into its module graph, and the class was unusable off a Vite bundler despite containing no browser code. The default is gone and the argument is required. Rejected alternatives: a `createSupabaseRepository(config)` factory reading a config object (core would then own a configuration format, and each platform reads configuration differently anyway), and a dependency-injection container (a framework to solve a one-argument problem). Authentication is explicitly *not* this class's responsibility - it uses whatever session the client it was handed carries, RLS remains the ownership check, and no service-role path exists.

**D298. `src/data/supabase/client.ts` stays in the web app permanently, and is now the only reader of configuration.** It keeps `VITE_SUPABASE_URL`, `VITE_SUPABASE_ANON_KEY`, `isSupabaseConfigured`, `createClient` and the browser session options (`persistSession`, `autoRefreshToken`, `detectSessionInUrl`) - all of which are browser-storage decisions a native client answers differently. `src/data/index.ts` is the one place that composes the two: `new SupabaseRepository(getSupabase())`. Core cannot ever hold this file: `import.meta` is a forbidden token in `platformNeutrality.test.ts`, and `import.meta.env` would not typecheck under `tsconfig.core.json`'s `types: []` regardless.

**D299. Backup orchestration takes a `Repository` argument rather than calling a global.** `exportBackup(repo)`, `importBackup(repo, backup, mode)` and `canReplaceImport(repo)` now live in `packages/core/src/data/backup.ts`. Which backend is active, and how the platform decided that, is not this module's business - it was the only reason the file could not move. The names were deliberately kept identical to the web app's, so Step 1.4 *deletes* the adapter rather than renaming call sites; distinct core names (`exportWorkspace` and friends) were considered and rejected for that reason. The transitional cost, two same-named functions of different arity, is confined to one file that aliases on import.

**D300. `src/data/backup.ts` survives as an adapter holding no logic.** Three one-line functions that pass `getRepository()` through. Duplicating any validation or persistence into the wrapper would defeat the move; refactoring `useBackup.ts` and `ImportExportSection.tsx` to pass a repository would be an early start on Step 1.4, whose whole subject is where hooks acquire one. Both files are untouched.
