Every P1-1 guarantee is preserved unchanged: structure validated before mutation, the referential `card.deckId` rule applied before any write with Merge consulting the library and Replace not, Dexie replace/merge still one real `rw` transaction, Supabase Replace still refused rather than emulated, and `ImportFailure`/`describeImportFailure` still never claiming more than the backend guaranteed. The deferred `replace_workspace` RPC was **not** written, and remains in `TODO.md`.

**D301. The fake PostgREST server stays a non-test module in core *source*, not under `packages/core/src/test/`.** It models row caps, exact counts, range pagination, generated columns, SQL three-valued NULL logic, failure injection and the Review RPC transaction, and reverting the repository to short-page termination fails 24 of the pagination cases - it is regression protection for P1-4 and P2-B, not a convenience. `platformNeutrality.test.ts` skips `*.test.ts` and the whole `src/test/` directory, and `tsconfig.core.json` excludes both as well, so parking it there would quietly exempt 380 lines from every guard the shipped code answers to. Where it now sits it is scanned for browser tokens, checked with no ambient types *and* with Node, and its `@supabase/supabase-js` import is checked against the manifest. It is deliberately absent from the barrel, and no fake, fixture or test symbol appears in any production chunk.

**D302. `backendParity.test.ts` stays in the web app, and reaches the fake by source path.** It is the one test that needs `DexieRepository` and `SupabaseRepository` at the same time, and Dexie must not move into core to satisfy a test. Extracting a shared parity fixture so each backend could have its own parity test was the plan's stated preference, and was rejected as building a framework for two callers - the file asserts `expect(remote).toEqual(local)` across 22 cases and only means anything with both backends in one process. Its import of `@itera/core/src/data/supabase/fakeSupabaseClient` is the single, test-only exception to the one-entry-point rule of D284; the alternative was exporting a 380-line test double from the production-facing barrel, which is worse. Production code still reaches only the barrel.

**D303. `@supabase/supabase-js` is declared in `packages/core/package.json`, even though core imports only types from it.** `platformNeutrality.test.ts` matches any `from '...'` specifier regardless of `import type`, by design: relying on the root app's hoisted copy is precisely the latent Metro failure the manifest check exists to prevent, and a `SupabaseClient` type that resolves today off a hoisted dependency is a package that does not build tomorrow in isolation. The version is the root's exact range (`^2.108.2`), so npm dedupes to one copy and the lockfile diff is a single line. Inventing local Supabase types to avoid the declaration was rejected: the shared backend being coupled to the official Supabase JS API is intentional and honest.

**D304. Core's tests import by relative path, which is enforcement rather than style.** Neither `tsconfig.core.json` nor `tsconfig.core.test.json` maps the `@/` alias, so a moved test that keeps an `@/domain/...` specifier runs green under Vitest and fails `tsc -b --force`. That second project earned its keep again here: `SupabaseRepository.test.ts` had been invisible to the compiler as a web test and failed on arrival, because `promise.catch((e) => e as ImportFailure)` is typed `void | ImportFailure` - every assertion on the error was reaching through a union that included `void`. `packages/core/src/test/rejection.ts` replaces the pattern with a typed `rejection<E>()` that also throws if a promise expected to reject resolves, which the old form silently tolerated.

**Verification.** 835 tests across 93 files, up from 831/93: four files left `src/data/supabase/` and four arrived in `packages/core/src/data/`, so the file count is unmoved and the split is now **38 core + 55 web**. The four new tests are all backup-orchestration coverage - that the passed `Repository` is the one actually read (two backends produce two different files), that `canReplaceImport` still reports `false` for the cloud backend, and that a refused Replace and an unresolved deck reference each reject with **zero table writes issued**. `backupCompleteness.test.ts` became that file and *lost* machinery: passing the repository in removed the `vi.mock` of the client module, the `vi.hoisted` holder, `vi.resetModules()` and the dynamic import. `npx tsc -b --force`, `npm run lint` and `npm run build` are clean.

A sabotage check confirmed the manifest guard is non-vacuous: deleting `@supabase/supabase-js` from `packages/core/package.json` fails `platformNeutrality.test.ts` naming **both** moved files as undeclared dependencies, and it was reverted. The forbidden-token sweep over `packages/core/src` returns only prose comments and the guard's own token list - zero runtime hits for `import.meta` and zero for `dexie` - and `grep` for imports from core back into `src/` returns nothing.

Browser pass in Chromium at 1440x900 and 390x844 against the local Dexie backend, on a disposable workspace, with **zero console errors and zero page errors** throughout: sign-in, Today, Library, Progress, Review history and Settings Import/Export all rendered at both viewports. The backup round trip is the evidence that matters here. Merge import of a six-card/two-deck file reported "Imported 6 cards, 2 decks, 1 drafts"; the Replace radio was offered and not `aria-disabled`, correct for a transactional backend. Grading one matching card auto-graded Correct, produced the FSRS rating row **1m / 6m / 10m / 8d**, and the graded review appeared in `/progress/history` with `Good 3` and a 10m interval. A subsequent export carried `{app: 'code-srs', version: 2, exportedAt}` over all five arrays and **two** review logs - the seeded one plus the new one - proving the grade persisted through the moved backend. Replace import then reported "Replaced everything with this backup" and history fell back to the file's single log, so Replace genuinely replaced. Supabase itself was **not** browser-verified: there is no live project, and none was created for this step; the shared implementation's evidence is the deterministic core suites only.

`npm run build` emitted a main chunk of 1,072.13 kB (gzip 304.45 kB) against the pre-change 1,072.04 kB (gzip 304.22 kB): **+0.09 kB raw, +0.23 kB gzipped**, accounted for by the adapter's three wrapper functions. The CSS hash is unchanged (`index-f7Qmf3Gv.css`), `npm ls @supabase/supabase-js` shows one deduped copy, and greps for `fakeSupabase`, `postgrestError`, `fixtureCard`, `maxRows`, `reportCount` and `failSelectAt` across every emitted chunk return nothing.

---

## 2026-08-23 - `@itera/core` takes ownership of repository registration and the TanStack Query hooks

Phase 1 Step 1.4 of the web + native convergence, and the keystone of it. Steps 1.1-1.3 moved contracts, the domain engine and the cloud backend; what was left was the layer that binds them - backend selection and the hooks. **No product behaviour changed.**

**D305. The Repository is reached through a registry, not a React context.** `packages/core/src/data/registry.ts` is `configureRepository(factory)` plus `getRepository()`, and both platforms use it identically: the entry point registers a factory at boot, and everything above the seam asks for the result. The alternative considered and rejected was a `RepositoryProvider` context. The registry wins on three counts that a context loses: every existing hook signature and call site stays byte-identical, no test tree or preview route has to grow a provider, and "never import a backend from a component, hook or page" stays literally true rather than becoming "except through a provider whose value is a backend". Five properties are load-bearing and each has a test: configuration takes a **factory** (so construction is deferrable), construction is **lazy**, the instance is **cached**, reconfiguration **drops** the cached instance, and use before configuration **throws**.

**D306. Core has no default backend, and that is the point.** `getRepository()` on an unconfigured registry throws rather than falling back to anything. A default would have to name a backend, and the entire reason for this indirection is that core cannot know which one it is running under. A loud failure at boot is strictly better than silently reading the wrong storage, which is the failure mode this replaces: the old `src/data/index.ts` picked Dexie whenever `VITE_SUPABASE_*` was blank, and the web test suite depended on that fallback *by omission* rather than by statement. `src/test/setup.ts` now says `configureRepository(() => new DexieRepository())` out loud, which is what it always meant.

**D307. Repository resolution moved from module scope into the query and mutation functions.** Every hook file used to open with `const repo = getRepository()`. That single line is what made importing a hook transitively construct a backend, and it is the reason the hooks could not be shared: a React Native bundle has no Dexie and no `import.meta.env`, so evaluating it would crash before any component rendered. Each `queryFn`/`mutationFn` now calls `getRepository()` itself. Two independent tests protect this, because either alone is weak: `packages/core/src/hooks/moduleScope.test.ts` imports every hook module *and the whole barrel* against an unconfigured registry and asserts no throw (plus a source scan proving the pattern is gone rather than merely unreachable), and `src/hooks/repositoryResolution.test.tsx` reconfigures the registry between renders and asserts a query hook and a mutation hook follow it. The last one is the direct inverse of the old bug: under a module-scope capture it fails.

**D308. The hooks are shared, not duplicated per platform.** The alternative was query keys plus plain async functions in core, with each app writing its own hooks over them. Rejected: it would duplicate `qk`, the invalidation policy, and `usePersistReviewResult`'s `updatedAt: log.reviewedAt` subtlety - the one that makes a retried commit byte-identical and therefore safe. Those are exactly the "meaningful cross-platform contract" the shared package exists to hold once. TanStack Query hooks are React but not DOM, so they run unmodified on native.

**D309. `QueryClient` stays with the application; only keys and hooks are shared.** Core owns `qk`, the queries, the mutations and the invalidation sets. Each platform constructs its own `QueryClient`, mounts its own `QueryClientProvider`, and later binds its own focus/online integration (`AppState`/NetInfo on native). Moving the client into core would have put a platform lifecycle concern inside a package that must not have one.

**D310. `react` and `@tanstack/react-query` are peer dependencies of `@itera/core`, never dependencies.** Both carry React context, and a second copy of either fails silently rather than loudly - a hook resolving against a different Query context simply never sees the data. A `dependencies` entry is precisely what would let npm install a second copy under `packages/core`. Declared as peers and supplied by each application, `npm ls` shows one deduped copy of each and `packages/core` has no `node_modules` of its own. `platformNeutrality.test.ts` was extended to read peers alongside dependencies - an extension, not a relaxation - and gained an assertion that these two are declared as peers specifically. The forbidden list is unchanged and still contains `react-dom`, `react-router`, `dexie` and every browser global: React is cross-platform, the DOM is not.

**D311. Backup keeps its explicit-repository primitives and gains one thin tier above them.** `exportBackup(repo)`, `importBackup(repo, backup, mode)` and `canReplaceImport(repo)` in `packages/core/src/data/backup.ts` are unchanged. Passing the repository in is what lets `packages/core/src/data/backup.test.ts` drive them against a Supabase double with no registry configured at all, and they remain the low-level primitive both platforms build on. `packages/core/src/hooks/useBackup.ts` adds `useImportBackup` plus two plain functions, `exportConfiguredBackup()` and `canReplaceConfiguredImport()`, which resolve the configured repository at call time. They are not hooks because export and the Replace capability are read during render rather than through a query, so a hook would buy nothing - `reviewWriteGuarantee()` in `useReview.ts` is the same shape for the same reason. What matters is that they resolve the repository *there* and not in a component. The layering is now `UI -> shared hook/helper -> getRepository() -> explicit-repo primitive`, and the temporary web adapter at `src/data/backup.ts` is deleted; `ImportExportSection.tsx` changed its import line and two call names, nothing else.

**D312. Core and the web app are separate Vitest projects, because sharing one setup was quietly undermining the boundary.** Once `src/test/setup.ts` installs fake-indexeddb and registers a Dexie repository, running core's tests through it means core - the code a native app also runs - is only ever proven under a web-specific bootstrap, with a browser storage shim present and a backend the package is not allowed to know about already configured. A core test that came to depend on either would pass here and fail under Metro, which is the exact class of bug the package boundary exists to catch. The core project therefore runs with **no setup file at all**: no fake IndexedDB, no `@` alias, no `VITE_*` env, no configured repository. The top-level `npx vitest run` still runs both, so there is no second command to remember. One test was placed by this rule rather than by convenience: `repositoryResolution.test.tsx` needs a React renderer, so it lives in the web project and core covers the import-time half instead.

**Verification.** 97 test files / 859 tests (core 41/494, web 56/365), up from 93/835; the +4 files and +24 tests are itemised in `CURRENT_STATE.md` §16 and are all new registry/resolution coverage. `npx tsc -b --force`, `npm run lint` and `npm run build` clean. `tsconfig.core.json` kept `lib: ["ES2023"]` and `types: []` - `skipLibCheck` absorbs the DOM references `@types/react` pulls in through the Query types, and the hook bodies name no DOM type themselves, so the enforcement did not have to be relaxed to admit React.

Browser pass in Chromium at 1440x900 and 390x844 against the local Dexie backend, on a disposable workspace, with **zero console errors and zero page errors** at both viewports. Boot reached `/login` with no unconfigured-Repository throw. A deck and a Recall card were created and read back out of IndexedDB directly rather than inferred from the page; Today then showed `1 card` due. Grading produced the FSRS rating row **1m / 6m / 10m / 8d**, advanced `reps` 0 -> 1 and `due` by 10 minutes, wrote **exactly one** ReviewLog carrying `stateBefore`, showed no persistence-failure state, and - the assertion that matters for retry safety - `card.updatedAt === log.reviewedAt`. Progress moved to `LEARNED 1 / REVIEWS 1 / CURRENT STREAK 1 day` and `/progress/history` carried the row as `Good 3, 10m`. Undo returned logs to 0 and `reps` to 0 with the card restored **verbatim** (JSON-identical to the pre-grade snapshot), so the transaction still inverts. Export wrote `{app: 'code-srs', version: 2}` over all five arrays; Merge reported "Imported 1 cards, 1 decks, 0 drafts" and Replace was offered - correct for a transactional backend - and reported "Replaced everything with this backup".

`npm ls react` and `npm ls @tanstack/react-query` each show a single deduped copy with `@itera/core` resolving to it; `packages/core/node_modules` does not exist. The production bundle contains no `fakeSupabase` and no backup fixtures.

## 2026-08-23 - `@itera/core` takes ownership of the authentication policy and session state

Phase 1 Step 1.5 of the web + native convergence. Steps 1.1-1.4 moved contracts, the domain engine, the cloud backend and the data layer; authentication was the last cross-platform *policy* living entirely in the web app, and the highest-consequence one - it holds the P1-3 mode rule and the P2-A bootstrap rule, both of which a second implementation would get wrong in the same way the first one did. **No product behaviour changed, and no Login copy changed.**

**D313. The mode rule is a pure function, and there is exactly one of it.** `resolveAuthState({ mode, supabaseSession, localSession })` in `packages/core/src/auth/resolveAuthState.ts` answers "who is signed in, and are they admitted?" and nothing else. It is the audit P1-3 invariant - the active backend decides which session is a session, and a record from the inactive mode is neither an identity nor an admission ticket - extracted from the middle of a `useMemo` where only a rendering React tree could reach it. It is now nine test cases in core's own project, each one a way the two auth models could be allowed to substitute for each other. Its documented asymmetry was preserved rather than tidied: in Supabase mode a session with no user email is authenticated but identity-less, because the app is not going to lock someone out over a missing profile field.

**D314. The state machine is framework-neutral; only the binding over it is React.** `createAuthEngine(config)` owns the whole lifecycle - the initial snapshot, the `getSession()` bootstrap with its `cancelled` flag and `then`/`catch`/`finally`, the `onAuthStateChange` subscription and its teardown, both local sign-ins, and sign-out. The alternative was moving `AuthProvider` across as a React component with hooks, which is what the Phase 0 plan sketched. It was rejected for one concrete reason: the behaviour worth protecting here is a *lifecycle*, and core's test project has no DOM and no renderer by deliberate design (D312). As a component, "a bootstrap that settles after unmount applies nothing" and "teardown unsubscribes" could only ever be tested in the web project, which is to say the shared code would be proven under a web-specific bootstrap - exactly what the separate project exists to prevent. As an engine with `getSnapshot`/`subscribe`/`start`, all of it is provable in core without mounting anything, and 19 of the 47 new core tests are that lifecycle.

**D315. Core owns `AuthProvider` and `useAuth` too, written with `createElement` so the package stays JSX-free.** The binding could have stayed in each app - it is about thirty lines of `useSyncExternalStore` and context. It did not, because thirty lines duplicated per platform is thirty lines that can drift, and every product component consuming `useAuth()` should be consuming the same hook a native app will. Keeping it in a `.ts` file rather than a `.tsx` is not stylistic: `tsconfig.core.json` has no `jsx` option and `platformNeutrality.test.ts` reads `.ts` sources off disk, so a `.tsx` in core would have required relaxing the compiler configuration *and* widening the guard, and would have been silently unscanned in the window between. One `createElement` call for a component that renders exactly one element is the smaller price. `useSyncExternalStore` is what makes the snapshot binding correct rather than merely working, which is why the engine caches its snapshot and returns the same object until something actually changes - a test asserts that directly.

**D316. `LocalSessionStore` is a seam for auth session storage and nothing else, and it is synchronous.** Three methods, `read`/`write`/`clear`, with `write` carrying the `remember` flag. It is not a general persistence port and no other state belongs behind it - a "storage abstraction" that grew to cover preferences and caches would be a second data layer beside the `Repository`, which is the one this project already has. Synchronous was a decision, not an oversight: the first auth snapshot is computed during render, and that is precisely what guarantees no frame exists in which a stale record renders the app authenticated (D252). An async read would have reintroduced that frame to serve a platform that does not need it - native is always in Supabase mode, so it never reads a local session at all and can supply a store that does nothing. The storage key stays platform-side: `'itera.session'` is in `src/auth/localSession.ts`, and core never learns it.

**D317. The auth configuration is a provider prop, not a second registry.** `configureRepository` is a registry because the repository is reached from hundreds of call sites that must not thread it through. Auth has exactly one consumer of its configuration - the provider at the top of the tree - so a global would have bought nothing and cost something: two module-level registries that each platform must initialise in the right order, with a standing question about what happens if one is configured and the other is not. `AuthConfig` is a discriminated union on `mode`, so "a Supabase client is required in Supabase mode" and "there is nothing to reach for in local mode" are compiler errors rather than runtime branches, and the client arrives as a getter so core never triggers its construction and never builds one.

**D318. One platform decision, two configured shared systems, and product code asks `useAuth()` rather than the environment.** `src/main.tsx` reads `isSupabaseConfigured` once into `cloudEnabled` and uses it for both `configureRepository(...)` and `createWebAuthConfig(...)`. It is now the **only** production module in the app that imports that predicate at all: `SignInPanel` and the settings Email & password section read `mode` from `useAuth()` instead. They were not wrong before - they read the same const - but "the environment" and "the active auth mode" being two names a component can choose between is how they came apart the first time. `createWebAuthConfig` takes the boolean as a parameter rather than reading it, so even the web adapter cannot make that decision on its own.

**D319. The web regression net was kept, assertion for assertion.** The Supabase-mode blocks in `RequireAuth.test.tsx` and `LoginPage.test.tsx` are the tests that failed against the pre-P1-3 provider, and they are the reason this step could be attempted at all. Every one of them still passes with **no edit to any assertion**; the only change in those files is that the render helper now builds an `AuthConfig` at render time, exactly as `main.tsx` builds one at boot, so the existing `isSupabaseConfigured`-as-a-getter mock keeps driving both modes from one file. Router guards, the Login screen and the browser session store stayed in the web app on purpose: `RequireAuth` encodes React Router behaviour, and the two platforms are expected to have *different sign-in interactions* - web magic link, native six-digit OTP - over identical session semantics. A shared Login component would have forced a symmetry the product does not want.

**D320. "Only one file may touch auth storage" is now enforced by a test rather than by a sentence.** `src/auth/storageIsolation.test.ts` scans the web sources and asserts that the set of non-test modules naming `localStorage`/`sessionStorage` is exactly the four that are meant to (the auth store, the theme preference, and the two one-shot reload throttles), that nothing under `src/auth/` but `localSession.ts` appears, and that the login, settings and layout surfaces contain none at all. The rule has been documented since the login screen shipped and had no mechanical backing; it is the web-side counterpart of `platformNeutrality.test.ts`, which stops the same thing happening on the other side of the boundary.

**Verification.** 101 test files / 914 tests (core 44/541, web 57/373), up from 97/859. The +4 files and +55 tests are all new coverage, itemised in `CURRENT_STATE.md` §16: 47 in core (10 + 12 + 25 across `resolveAuthState.test.ts`, `localSession.test.ts` and `authEngine.test.ts`), and 8 in web across the new storage-isolation guard and the `browserSessionStore` cases added to `localSession.test.ts`. `npx tsc -b --force`, `npm run lint` and `npm run build` clean. `tsconfig.core.json` was not touched: it still compiles core with `lib: ["ES2023"]` and `types: []`, and the auth sources name no DOM type. `platformNeutrality.test.ts` passes unchanged - no browser token, no `@/` import, no undeclared package - and needed no new dependency, since `@supabase/supabase-js` was already declared and `react` was already a peer.

Browser pass in Chromium at 1440x900 and 390x844, zero page errors at both. **Local mode:** a signed-out `/progress` reached `/login` and returned there after sign-in with the requested route preserved; Remember me wrote `itera.session` to `localStorage` only and survived a reload; the account menu read the email over "Stored in this browser"; the settings Email & password note read local-mode copy; sign out cleared both stores and returned to `/login`. **Demo:** entry landed on Today, wrote to `sessionStorage` only with `kind: 'demo'`, and the menu read "Demo workspace". **Supabase simulation** on the *same origin*, with the dev server restarted against a dummy project and a local session plus a sentinel deck planted first: the stale record was confirmed present before the app booted, `/progress` was never admitted, the record was cleared, `/login` was reachable in magic-link form with the password field and demo entry hidden, no "Stored in this browser" claim appeared anywhere, and the sentinel deck was read back out of IndexedDB **unchanged** - session cleanup only, as D251 requires.

P2-A was re-verified against a real failure rather than a mock, and the first attempt is worth recording because it looked like a regression and was not: with no stored GoTrue token, `getSession()` answers "no session" without touching the network, so no error is raised and none should be. Planting an expired token forces the refresh that actually fails. It then landed on `/login` after **27.9s desktop / 26.0s phone** of supabase-js's own internal backoff - the same ~27s D262 recorded, and still not Itera's to shorten - with `loading` resolved, the stale record cleared, the exact sentence "Couldn't reach the account service. Check your connection and try again." in the existing `role="alert"` region, no transport internals in it, and the form still usable.

## 2026-08-23 - `@itera/core` takes ownership of the text syntax and the brand tokens, and the content boundary is audited

Phase 1 Step 1.6 of the web + native convergence. Steps 1.1-1.5 moved contracts, the domain engine, the cloud backend, the data layer and the auth policy. What was left in a web component was the **meaning of a card's text** - three regexes interleaved with JSX in `RichText.tsx`, with no test file at all - and the locked palette, which existed only as CSS custom properties a React Native app cannot read. The step also did what the extraction made possible: audited the content boundary with real malicious payloads rather than asserting that React escapes strings. **No visual changed and no syntax changed.**

**D321. The parser moved; the renderer did not.** `packages/core/src/content/parseRichText.ts` owns `parseRichText`/`parseRichInline` and returns a closed union of semantic nodes (`paragraph`/`code`; `text`/`strong`/`em`/`inlineCode`). `src/components/text/RichText.tsx` keeps both exports and every class string and is now a pure node-to-JSX mapper. The split is at the smallest clean seam: `splitBlocks` was already pure, and `inline`/`emphasis` were pure logic that happened to allocate React elements at the end. A renderer that emits DOM elements is web-specific by definition, so the argument that "React is cross-platform, move the component" was rejected - what must not exist twice is the *interpretation*, not the elements. `InlineText` was checked for being a second parser and is not one; it always called the same `inline()`, so there was nothing to consolidate there.

**D322. The rules a native re-implementation would get wrong are now tests, not prose.** The parser had **zero** tests before this step. Twenty-eight now cover it, and three of them are the whole reason the extraction mattered: underscores are never emphasis markers (`snake_case` and `__dunder__` stay literal), inline code beats emphasis with its contents literal, and `**` is matched before `*`. Two more lock behaviour that was accidental-looking and is load-bearing: a prose run of only blank lines is dropped while a run of spaces is kept, and repeated parses of the same string agree - the module-level `FENCE` regex is global, so a missing `lastIndex` reset would make the second parse differ from the first.

**D323. `plainLabel` folded into core as `stripInlineMarkers`, with its exact semantics preserved.** The Matching board's accessible-name flattening was a third piece of content semantics living in a component. It moved verbatim - a blind removal of every backtick and asterisk, matched or not - rather than being rewritten as a parse-derived flattening, which would be more principled and would differ on unmatched markers. Changing what a screen reader announces is a product decision and does not get made as a side effect of a refactor; the module comment says so, so the option stays open rather than being quietly foreclosed.

**D324. Design tokens went into `packages/core/src/design/tokens.ts`, not a `packages/tokens`.** The Phase 0 plan proposed a second workspace package. There was no structural reason for one: core is already the single shared package, has no `exports` map, and gained one more barrel section at zero cost, while a second package would have added a manifest, a resolution path and a second thing every future bundler configuration has to know about. Deviation from the plan, taken deliberately and on the project owner's instruction to keep early structure small.

**D325. CSS stays the source; a bidirectional drift test is the seam, and no build step exists.** Tailwind v4 here is CSS-first (`@theme inline`), so feeding TypeScript values into it would need a generated stylesheet or a PostCSS plugin - a watch process standing in front of `index.css`, whose semantic re-point mechanism is explicitly documented as not to be "simplified away". Instead `src/design/tokenDrift.test.ts` parses the `.itera-scope` block and the `--radius-itera-*` declarations and asserts equality with the module **in both directions**: an edited hex fails, and so does a new `--itera-*` variable with no shared counterpart. Both directions were verified by perturbation. The two shadow values are the only exemption, because a CSS `box-shadow` string and React Native's elevation model are different models rather than different spellings - the intent *names* are shared instead - and the test asserts that exemption set is exactly those two, so it cannot silently absorb a third.

**D326. What was deliberately not tokenized.** The type and spacing scale stayed out: `design-system.md` already calls it a design rule rather than a token, and there is no mirrored set of CSS variables for it to be the counterpart of. Page widths, breakpoints, nav heights and sidebar widths stayed out too. Sharing identity is the goal; sharing layout mechanics is the pixel-parity trap the convergence plan exists to avoid, and a test asserts no token key names one.

**D327. The content-security audit found no stored-XSS defect, and that finding is now mechanically maintained.** A repo-wide search for raw-HTML and code-evaluation sinks across `src` and `packages` returned exactly one hit, and it was a comment. Itera supports no user-authored links and no images-in-markdown, so the text path has no URL sink at all. Rather than record that as a paragraph that decays, `src/components/text/renderingSinks.test.ts` reads every production module in `src/` and fails on `dangerouslySetInnerHTML`, `innerHTML`, `outerHTML`, `insertAdjacentHTML`, `document.write`, `DOMParser`, `eval(` or `new Function(` - the same shape as `storageIsolation.test.ts` (D320). This is the reason no permanent "audit XSS someday" item was added to `TODO.md`: the finding is enforced, not remembered.

**D328. Safe rendering, never sanitization - content is preserved byte for byte.** The suites assert both halves at once, and they pull in opposite directions on purpose: an attack string must come back out of storage unchanged, *and* must be inert when displayed. A flashcard app for software engineering whose store mangles generics, C++ templates, comparison operators or a SQL star-select is broken, so stripping characters was never an acceptable answer. Verified through the real import path into the real local backend: hostile text round-trips identically, and a lone unmatched asterisk specifically survives, which a greedier emphasis rule or a naive sanitizer would eat.

**D329. `WalkthroughInteraction.image` was the one real finding: a beacon, not a script sink, and it is now closed.** It is the only card field that becomes a URL the app loads, and `validateBackupEntities.ts` never inspected it. It is not classic XSS - a `javascript:` URL is inert in an `<img src>`, and SVG loaded through `<img>` runs no script - but an imported backup could point it at a remote host, so opening a flashcard called out to whoever wrote the file, leaking IP and user agent and confirming the card was opened, in an app whose premise is that a local workspace works offline. `isSafeImageSource` (`packages/core/src/content/imageSource.ts`) now accepts only base64 `data:` URLs of an explicitly listed raster type. Three enforcement points, deliberately redundant: the file picker, backup validation (so it never persists), and the Review renderer (so a row already in the store from before the rule renders no `<img>` at all). Whitespace is refused outright rather than normalized, because a browser strips ASCII whitespace out of a URL attribute before parsing it - a media type split across a newline would become a different type after parsing than a lenient check saw.

**D330. SVG was excluded, and authoring was narrowed to match rather than left broader than the rule.** `accept="image/*"` and a `startsWith('image/')` paste filter accepted SVG on both authoring paths, so excluding it is a real restriction, not a no-op. It was taken anyway: SVG is markup with an XML parser and an external-reference model attached, substantially more active content than a raster format, and the product has no requirement for it. The alternative - allowing SVG in authoring but refusing it on import - would have produced a card the learner could create and then not re-import from their own backup. AVIF is *included*, because `<img>` support is universal in current browsers and it carries no active content, so omitting it would be a usability regression with no security gain. The image bytes are never decoded or re-encoded; the boundary is the source format alone.

**D331. Every security assertion was checked against the unfixed code.** Introducing a single `dangerouslySetInnerHTML` into the text renderer fails **25 of 53** cases across the three renderer suites and the sink scan. Removing the image guard from the renderer and the validator fails 3. Perturbing one token, and deleting one token, each fail the drift test in the direction they should. None of these suites is a snapshot: they inspect the live DOM for injected elements, `on*` attributes and hostile URLs, and count the author's angle brackets to prove none was consumed into markup.

**Verification.** 109 test files / 1031 tests (core 47/590, web 62/441), up from 101/914 - **+8 files, +117 tests**, all new coverage. `npx tsc -b --force`, `npm run lint` and `npm run build` clean; no dependency added, and no sanitizer library reaches the bundle. Core stayed `.ts`-only, so `platformNeutrality.test.ts` and the core Vitest include still scan `.ts` and the `.tsx` blind spot Step 1.5 reported remains open and unwidened. The design tokens are tree-shaken out of the production bundle entirely (only a test consumes them); the main chunk moved 1,073.22 kB to 1,074.39 kB (gzip 305.32 to 305.87), which is the image predicate plus the node allocations the parser now returns.

Browser pass in Chromium at 1440x900 and 390x844, both in a throwaway context with every non-origin request intercepted and recorded. A disposable workspace was imported through the real Import UI (8 cards, 2 decks) and every surface visited - Today, Library, both deck pages, all six study previews, `/review` for both decks, `/preview`, Progress and Review history - with the answer face revealed on each. **Zero external requests, zero console errors, zero page errors, and the sentinel probe never set.** No element in any live DOM carried an `on*` attribute, a `javascript:` href or a remote-host source, and no `<script>` was created from card content; the payloads rendered as visible literal text on all six interactions plus Tip and Explanation. The normal cards were unchanged: bold, italic, inline-code pills and real CodeMirror blocks with syntax highlighting, and the C++ template, generic, nested-generic, comparison-operator, snake_case, dunder and quoted-SQL cases all rendered literally at both widths. The beacon backup was refused at import with the field named, the accepted formats listed, and "nothing was changed" stated.

One observation, not a regression: a single unbroken ~200-character token - which only an artificial payload produces - overflows the card prompt horizontally at 390px, because `CardPrompt` sets `whitespace-pre-wrap` without a word-break. No real card content reaches that, and it predates this step.

## 2026-08-23 - `@itera/core` takes ownership of interaction behavior; each platform keeps its own View binding

Phase 1 Step 1.7 of the web + native convergence, and the last shared-code extraction of Phase 1. Context in [`mobile_app_conversion/master_plan.md`](mobile_app_conversion/master_plan.md) §5.

**D332. `InteractionDefinition` was one interface doing two incompatible jobs, and it is now two.** It carried `type`, `interactive`, `isResponseReady`, `autoGrade` and `widthFor` - all plain data functions - alongside `View: ComponentType<...>`, a React DOM component. A native app could therefore only import web Views, which is impossible, or re-author the behavior, which is the silent-divergence failure the shared package exists to prevent: a second `autoGrade` changes what the learner is told without failing anything. `packages/core/src/interactions/types.ts` now declares `InteractionBehavior<T>` with the five behavioral fields, and `src/features/reviewV2/interactions/types.ts` declares `WebInteractionDefinition<T> extends InteractionBehavior<T> { View }`. The six behavior objects moved to `packages/core/src/interactions/<type>.ts` **verbatim** - the same delegations to `gradeMultipleChoice`, `matchesAcceptedAnswer`, `gradeOrdering`, `gradeMatching` and `aggregateWalkthroughResult`, the same readiness predicates, the same "Recall has no grader at all". No semantics changed.

**D333. The web binding composes; it does not restate.** Each `interactions/<type>/index.ts` is now `{ ...<type>Behavior, View: <Type>View }`. That shape is what makes the invariant checkable: `src/features/reviewV2/interactions/registry.test.ts` asserts each registered definition's `interactive`, `isResponseReady`, `autoGrade` and `widthFor` are the **same references** as core's exported object. A field-by-field copy would typecheck, behave identically today, and pass any value-based test - reference equality is the only assertion that catches it, the same technique `coreSurface.test.ts` uses on the `@/domain` shims (D294). A grep for those four names under `src/features/reviewV2/interactions/` now returns only `types.ts`'s contract and one *call* of the shared readiness rule.

**D334. `widthFor` is shared because it names an intent, not a measurement.** It returns `'default' | 'wide'`, and the mapping to `max-w-2xl`/`max-w-4xl` stays in `ReviewSessionScreen`. Core contains no rem, px or class string, so a native app is free to decide that `'wide'` means a horizontally scrolling board or a different layout entirely rather than 896px. The rule it encodes - a three-column Matching board does not fit a normal flashcard column - is a fact about the content, which is why it belongs with the behavior; how much room that translates to is a fact about the platform, which is why it does not.

**D335. `WalkthroughState` moved to core with the behavior that reads it.** `walkthroughBehavior`'s readiness ("every step answered") and its card-level aggregate both interpret the walkthrough's response object, so leaving that type beside the web View would have left the shared behavior depending on a web module. It is pure data - a step index plus two id-keyed records - and `src/features/reviewV2/interactions/walkthrough/state.ts` is deleted; the three importers take it from `@itera/core`.

**D336. `WalkthroughView` now calls the shared readiness rule instead of mirroring it.** Its Finish-button enablement was a local `interaction.steps.every((s) => s.id in state.answers)` whose own comment said it "mirrors isResponseReady exactly". Two copies of a rule that must agree is the same defect class as a duplicated grader, just smaller, so the View calls `walkthroughBehavior.isResponseReady(state, interaction)`. The behaviors are declared with `satisfies` rather than a type annotation precisely so each literal keeps its own type and the call needs no `?.`.

**D337. One registry per platform, and none in core.** A behavior-only registry in core was considered and rejected: it would have had no consumer, because every real lookup needs the View too, and making it a total `Record` would have turned the deliberate runtime failure into a compile error. So core exports six named behavior objects and no lookup table; `interactions/registry.ts` stays the one web registry, still `Partial<{ [T in InteractionType]: WebInteractionDefinition<T> }>`, still throwing at runtime for an unregistered type. A future native registry binds the same six objects to native Views and must keep the same throw. The guard was **mutation-tested**: deleting `if (!def) throw` fails the registry suite's throw case and leaves the other eight green, so the test discriminates rather than merely passing.

**D338. `InteractionViewProps` and `ReviewPhase` deliberately stayed web-side.** `InteractionViewProps` is the contract between the review shell and a React DOM component, and includes `hideActions`, an affordance that exists only because the web card editor has a simplified authoring preview (D71). A native View will want its own event signatures over the same learning state, so sharing the prop bag would have exported a web decision as a cross-platform contract. `ReviewPhase` stayed for a simpler reason: **no behavior function takes a phase**, so nothing forced the phase machine across the boundary. Moving it would have been scope creep justified only by aesthetics, and Step 1.7's scope was the behavior split.

**Verification.** 111 test files / 1064 tests (core 48/614, web 63/450), up from 109/1031 - **+2 files, +33 tests**, both new: 24 core behavior tests and 9 web registry tests. The core suite tests *wiring*, not grading, because each grader already has an exhaustive file and a second copy of those cases would be a second thing to update. `npx tsc -b --force`, `npm run lint` and `npm run build` clean; no dependency added; the main chunk moved 1,074.39 kB to 1,074.44 kB (gzip 305.87 to 305.95), which is comment and module-boundary noise. Core stayed `.ts`-only - Views are web-specific, so nothing about a behavior contract wanted JSX - and `platformNeutrality.test.ts` needed no change: it already scans every new file and confirms `packages/core/src/interactions/` imports only core types and core graders.

Core's own tests being typechecked (D286) caught two real defects in the new suite that would otherwise have been silent: an `InteractionBehavior<never>` cast that no behavior is assignable to, and calls passing an interaction to the two readiness rules whose shared signature takes only a response. Both were fixed by writing what is true rather than by widening a type.

Browser pass in Chromium at 1440x900 and 390x844 against a disposable seven-card workspace imported through the real Import UI. All six interactions were driven end to end in production `/review` - Recall revealed with Space and rated; Multiple Choice, Write Code, Ordering and both Matching boards built and submitted; Walkthrough answered step by step to Finish and one card-level rating - for 7 of 7 cards at each width, with **zero console errors and zero page errors**. `widthFor` was observed both ways: the three-column Matching card renders in the wide column and the two-column one in the standard column, in `/review` and at `/cards/:id/study`. All six `/design-preview/review/*` routes render their own interaction, and the authoring live previews still mount the production View at both widths (behind the narrow layout's Preview tab at 390px). Nothing moved visually.

## 2026-08-23 - the web application moves into `apps/web`; the root becomes workspace orchestration

**D339. The root stopped being an application.** Until now `package.json` was simultaneously the workspace root and the web package, so one hoisted `node_modules` carried both Vite's dependency set and, eventually, React Native's. That is the structure the Phase 0 report rejected as option B: it makes a second app a rehoist rather than an addition, and it lets a future Metro resolve `react-dom` or `dexie` from a mobile import. The root now declares `workspaces: ["apps/*", "packages/*"]`, delegating `dev`/`build`/`preview` scripts, and only the genuinely repository-wide gates (`typescript`, `oxlint`, `vitest`, `playwright`, `@types/node`). Every web dependency moved to `apps/web/package.json`, which is also what supplies the `react` and `@tanstack/react-query` that satisfy `@itera/core`'s peer declarations - one physical copy of each, verified with `npm ls` and on disk.

**D340. This was a relocation, and the diff proves it.** 306 of the 312 relocated files are `git mv` renames with **zero content change** (R100). The six that changed are the minimum the new depth requires: `apps/web/tsconfig.app.json` and `tsconfig.node.json` (`extends` and `tsBuildInfoFile` gain `../../`), `apps/web/vite.config.ts` (one added `envDir` line), and three tests described in D343. Nothing was copied, nothing was left duplicated at the root, and no export was renamed. The test count is identical on both sides of the move - 111 files, 1064 tests, split 63/450 web and 48/614 core exactly as before - because no test changed package.

**D341. `packages/core` was deliberately not touched, including its `tsconfig`s.** No file moved into or out of the package, and `tsconfig.core.json` / `tsconfig.core.test.json` stay at the repository root even though the two web configs moved into `apps/web`. The asymmetry is intentional and the reason is mechanical, not aesthetic: the web configs *must* be colocated, because `include: ["src"]` and `paths: {"@/*": ["./src/*"]}` are resolved relative to the config file. Core's configs address `packages/core/src` from the root and gain nothing by moving, while moving them would be a change to a package this milestone exists to leave alone. Revisit only if a concrete tooling need appears.

**D342. Environment lookup did not move with the app.** `apps/web/vite.config.ts` sets `envDir` to the repository root, resolved from the config file's own directory (`path.resolve(__dirname, '../..')`) rather than from `process.cwd()`, so it is identical whether Vite is launched by the root script or by `npm run dev --workspace @itera/web`. `.env.local.example` stays at the root and no environment file was moved or renamed. The alternative - letting Vite default `envDir` to `apps/web` - would have silently stopped reading a developer's existing repo-root `.env.local`, turning a cloud-configured checkout into a local-Dexie one with no error. A structural milestone is the wrong place to change what configures the backend.

**D343. A source-scanning test must resolve from `import.meta.url`, never `process.cwd()`.** `tokenDrift.test.ts`, `renderingSinks.test.ts` and `storageIsolation.test.ts` each read the web source tree off disk. All three used `path.resolve(process.cwd(), 'src')`, and the runner is invoked from the repository root while those files now live under `apps/web` - so they would have scanned a directory that does not exist. Their failure mode matters more than the fix: a guard pointed at nothing finds no violations and **passes**, which is worse than not existing. They now resolve from the test file. Verified by probe rather than by inspection: a throwaway module containing `innerHTML` and `localStorage` was added to `apps/web/src`, both guards failed and named it, and it was removed. Core's two scanners (`platformNeutrality.test.ts`, `moduleScope.test.ts`) already carried a two-candidate lookup that still resolves and were left alone.

**D344. Tailwind's source scope narrowed, and 1.6 kB of dead CSS went with it.** Tailwind v4 auto-detects source files from the Vite root. That root was the repository, so Tailwind had been reading class names out of `docs/*.md`, `CLAUDE.md` and `AGENTS.md` and emitting utilities for them; it is now `apps/web`. The compiled stylesheet dropped 68.43 kB -> 66.79 kB and 20 bare utilities (`rounded-lg`, `sticky`, `underline`, `opacity-50`, `text-green`, `grid-cols-[...]` - the ellipsis is literal, which is the tell). This was *proven* rather than assumed: rebuilding with `@source '../../..'` reproduced the pre-move stylesheet's exact content hash, the removed set was diffed, and every entry was checked against every quoted token in the web source. None is used as a standalone class, and each variant form the app does render (`hover:underline`, `disabled:opacity-50`, `focus-visible:ring-offset-2`) is still emitted. No visual change; the app simply stopped shipping CSS for class names that only ever appeared in prose. JS is unchanged in size and the PWA precache is unchanged at 24 entries.

**D345. `vercel.json` gained `outputDirectory`, and that is a claim about the dashboard we cannot verify.** The build output moved from `dist/` to `apps/web/dist/`, so the committed config now says so, keeping the existing SPA rewrite untouched and adding nothing else - no `buildCommand`, no framework override. This assumes the Vercel project's **Root Directory remains the repository root**. If the dashboard is instead set to Root Directory `apps/web`, the two configurations contradict each other and must be reconciled before deploying, because in that setup the output directory would normally be plain `dist`. Deployment was **not** verified: the dashboard is not visible from the repository.
